## Session 75 Reflection — 2026-04-24 (map-reader-llm)

A long session — 16 commits landed on main — that began as a
straightforward continuation of Step 4 (level-up the interim-doc
scorecard), acquired three unplanned threads (physical move of the
Obs 235 retracted probe; factor-analysis 512 px → Phase 2b N=1
relabel; a 4-script aggregator-hardening close-out), and ended with
a continuity-doc update and a reflection. Across 7 Step-4 items and
a /audit pass, the fresh-context verifier caught 2–3 errors per
item, at least one of which was load-bearing every time. Items 1–7
of the scorecard are now DONE; items 8–14 remain for the next
session, with item 8 (Obs 268 crosstab level-up) pre-scoped as the
entry point.

### Prompt: "What surprised you about this session?"

Two things. The first surprise was a latent one: the Obs 235
retraction had been in working-notes for seven months, but the
retracted-probe data itself — 7,977 tracked files under
`outputs/h10/{consensus, evaluation, verified, verifier-crops, wbf}/`
and 9 files under `results/h10/` — had remained in the active tree
unflagged. The scorecard §3.11 itself pointed at
`sweep_results.json` and `verifier_independence_probe.md` as
authoritative sources for the h10 synthesis. If Shawn hadn't
maintained the "archive never delete" instinct and the verifier
pattern had caught the scope-level issue on the first pass, the
paper would have cited retracted data as evidence of a library-
composition null. Retraction-in-prose does not guarantee
retraction-in-filesystem. This pattern — a known retraction's
physical footprint not being cleaned up — is the kind of thing that
compounds. The move to `archive/h10-h12-v1-retracted-probe/` with a
README plus an ARCHIVE-MANIFEST entry plus a working-notes follow-up
is the corrective, but the underlying lesson is that retractions
need a filesystem audit, not just a prose entry.

The second surprise was that one bug turned out to be two bugs in
the factor-analysis output. Two rows labelled "T=0.7 vs T=1.0
(512 px text/image)" had blank labels and zero F1 values — initially
framed as "data-completeness gap" in the scorecard. Option A
investigation revealed (a) the aggregator was reading `global_a` and
`global_b` from permutation JSONs that use `condition_a` and
`condition_b` — a silent schema mismatch that had zero-filled the
two rows — AND (b) the "512 px" label was wrong; the source
`condition_a.source.geojson` resolved to
`outputs/retest/phase2b/track{1-image,2-text}/T{0.7,1.0}/run_1/` —
i.e., 384 px Phase 2b retest single-pass, not 512 px. Same bug
produced two distinct errors (a data-loss error and a
mislabelling error) at the same rows. Hardening the script, fixing
the JSON/CSV/MD, and relabelling took one commit; the pattern
generalises to any aggregator that reads by a hard-coded key name.
Agent D's sweep flagged three other scripts with the same pattern
shape (no confirmed data loss, but the failure mode exists) — all
hardened in close-out.

### Prompt: "What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?"

Three.

1. **Why a single dry-run can erase a hand-authored narrative**.
   The `collect-factor-analysis.py` script's `write_outputs` function
   regenerates `factor_analysis_results.md` as an auto-generated
   tables-only output. The Item-7 level-up replaced that file's
   content with a 390-line paper-citation narrative (exec summary +
   methods + caveats + paper implications) at the same path. Then
   the Commit 1 dry-run of the hardened script overwrote the
   narrative back to 91-line tables. Reverting from git, splitting
   the output path so auto goes to
   `factor_analysis_results_autogen.md`, and adding a schema-
   lockstep comment and a guardrail in the continuity doc fixed it.
   A future reader seeing the split path without this context may
   not understand why it exists — "why does this script write to a
   non-obvious name?" The answer is "because the obvious name is a
   hand-authored paper artefact that would otherwise be destroyed
   on every re-run".
2. **Why the sign in h10 analysis_summary line 97 looked wrong**.
   Agent B flagged the headline table at line 36 (`+0.005 pool_020
   vs pool_160`) as a sign error. Direct check: the table is
   consistent with the h8-v2 / h12-v2 convention `ΔF1 = F1_a − F1_b`
   and with the permutation JSON. Line 97 narrative, however, said
   "ΔF1 = −0.005 pool_020 minus pool_160" — the arithmetic is wrong
   if "pool_020 minus pool_160" is taken literally (0.727 − 0.722 =
   +0.005). The fix was to line 97, not line 36. Agent B was half-
   right: there was a sign inconsistency, but it was in the
   narrative, not the table. A future reader seeing only the diff
   would think "agent caught sign error in headline table" — the
   actual lesson is "agent was wrong about which location had the
   bug; main-thread verification caught this". This is exactly the
   sub-agent-verdict-as-draft pattern that Session 74 named.
3. **The metrics_master.csv vs metrics_master.json drift is pre-
   existing, not a Session-75 regression**. The CSV has 4 rows
   (pro-high-text pool_size=10) that aren't in the JSON. Both
   committed at HEAD. During the Commit 4 dry-run of
   `consolidate_paper_metrics.py`, the regenerated CSV had 100 rows
   (matching JSON) — the 4 extra rows are from an earlier run that
   was committed alongside a more-recently-regenerated JSON. The
   close-out commit logged this as a Step 6 polish-pass item;
   deliberate regeneration during paper finalisation will bring
   them to parity. A future reader investigating the drift may
   wonder "who introduced this?" — the answer is "pre-existing,
   surfaced during hardening dry-run, deferred per the plan's
   output-regeneration non-goal".

### Prompt: "What was different about this session compared to recent ones?"

Scale and discipline. Session 73 was Steps 1–3 (warm-up, doc review,
meta-findings synthesis). Session 74 was Steps 1–3 again (mostly
the scorecard, meta-findings, and a CRS bugfix). Session 75 was 7
full Step-4 items (five L-effort syntheses, two M-effort reports,
and a level-up) plus the close-out pass. Each Step-4 item followed the
same structure: load context → extract headline numbers → draft →
dispatch verifier → apply corrections → commit. The pattern
stabilised around item 2 and held through item 7; the close-out
added a /audit pass and four aggregator-hardening commits on top.

Discipline emerged around three habits:

1. **Checkpoint-commit after each item** rather than batch. 15 of 16
   Session-75 commits were ≤ 1 item of work, and a third of them
   (the close-out) were ≤ 1 file of work. This kept the reviewable-
   unit small and made the verifier-catch rate visible: 2–3 errors
   per item, every item.
2. **Verify the verifier**. Agent B flagged 3 Priority-1 issues;
   one was a sign-convention misread (the bug was elsewhere). Main-
   thread direct-filesystem check confirmed which 2 were real and
   the 3rd was a verifier error. This is the Session-74 lesson
   operationalised — sub-agent outputs are drafts, including
   verifier outputs, and the verification-of-verifier layer caught
   1 of 3 agent verdicts this session.
3. **Plan-mode for the close-out**. The /audit skill found 8
   hardening items in one script; Agent D surfaced 3 risk patterns
   in three more; the doc hygiene items added two more. Rather than
   fold them all into one sprawling commit, the user invoked plan
   mode and I drafted a 6-commit sequence. The plan file is at
   `~/.claude/plans/i-d-like-to-clear-snazzy-pie.md`; the plan was
   approved and all 6 commits landed in the expected sequence.

A distinctive relational note: Shawn's question "can we either
correct the source JSON or put this known error into a readme in
the folder? where is it currently flagged?" triggered the Option A
investigation that uncovered the 512 px → Phase 2b N=1 mislabelling.
Without that question, my level-up would have committed a caveat
around the blanks and moved on. The question's form — "can we fix
this or document it" — invited investigation of which; the default
bias-to-document would have missed the underlying bug. Noting
because the form was productive in a way "what about the blanks?"
would not have been.

**Session**: 2026-04-24, map-reader-llm. 16 commits (`f6d1cdb4`
through `55c5c82c`), pushed to `origin/main`. 11 Explore / general-
purpose agent dispatches (7 per-item verifiers, 4 parallel close-out
sweeps) — none had final-say verdict authority; the verification-of-
verifier pattern held throughout.
**Key moment**: Shawn's question "we re-ran these retracted runs" —
which turned out to be partially true (not an h10-framed re-run but
coverage via H8 v2 + H12 v2) — triggered the 2-agent background
sweep that resolved the scope question. The filesystem-audit
discipline that followed (moving 7,988 files to
`archive/h10-h12-v1-retracted-probe/`) was a direct consequence.
**Pattern worth naming**: "retraction-in-prose ≠ retraction-in-
filesystem". Add to the guardrails list when a future retraction
happens.

## Session 76 Reflection — 2026-04-24/25 (map-reader-llm)

An overnight autonomous session. Items 8–14 of Step 4 (7 items covering
16 documents, including 8 script-hardenings and 2 new synthesis docs);
plus a Batch A Session-77 follow-up pass (8 image-vs-text paired
permutation tests + 6 extended-buffer evaluations on sapphire); plus a
Batch B1 Step-5 mark-superseded sweep (17 files moved + SUPERSEDED
banners + 6 new themed archive subdirs). ~24 commits across two sittings
(the 18-commit Session-76-proper + Batch A's 3 commits + Batch B1's 2).

### Prompt: "What surprised you about this session?"

Three things.

First, **the cross-pipeline context bleed from Targets 4 and 5**. I
initially wrote "Gemini 2.5 Pro" as the proposer model for the phase3a
text-matrix and secondary-effects (image track) docs during the Item 10
batch, carried over from a half-remembered context. The error was mine,
not the dossier agent's. It surfaced later when I wrote Target 6's
consensus-analysis-summary — which has "Gemini 3 Flash" in its own
header — and I cross-referenced the MCC batch summary (`batch_mcc_summary.md`
rows "Flash HIGH text 26-of-30" and "Flash HIGH image 6-of-10"). The
cross-reference caught the error. Retrospective fix landed in commit
`82254d16` correcting both Target 4 and Target 5 headers + narrative +
suggested paper text. This is the Session 75 "Added 2026-04-24 item 2"
guardrail (cross-pipeline context bleed) being exercised successfully —
but I should notice it on the first write, not the third. The prompt
"does the model string in my header match a cross-artefact row label?"
belongs in the pre-write checklist for any level-up that touches a
multi-track analysis tree.

Second, **the new-synthesis-doc verifier catch-rate**. Items 12 (cross-
track comparison) and 13 (limitations consolidation) were both new
synthesis docs (not additive level-ups of existing tables). The
verifier-agent pass on item 12 caught **4 Priority-1 errors**:
(a) vote_t asymmetry across tracks (image = 3, text = 4; I had claimed
parity); (b) 472 vs 474 phantom-TP conflation; (c) §4 "n human-reviewed"
row confused 555 FP count with a reviewer count; (d) rounding
inconsistency. Item 13 had only 2 Priority-2s. Item 12 was more
confabulation-prone because it invented a new synthesis table structure
and had to pull numbers from multiple sibling artefacts; item 13
structurally mirrored Agent 3's dossier with cross-verified Obs/errata
anchors. Lesson: new synthesis docs need full verifier passes and
systematic cross-referencing against `resolved_config.yaml` for pipeline-
control parity, not just against the evaluation JSONs.

Third, **the sapphire-compute workflow with a transient feature branch
was cleaner than I expected**. The user wanted origin/main untouched
until they reviewed Session 76 in the morning, but sapphire needed the
updated `evaluate_detections.py` (metadata-embed upgrade). I pushed a
`s76-sapphire-compute` branch (disposable), sapphire pulled it, ran the
compute, rsync'd results to amd-tower, sapphire restored its prior state
(`git stash pop`), the transient branch was deleted from both sides,
origin/main stayed frozen at `8f213c67`. The end-state is: all data on
local main, origin/main untouched, sapphire back to its pre-compute
state. Worth keeping as a named pattern: "transient-branch compute
offload" — cheaper than pushing main or dealing with dirty sapphire
working trees.

### Prompt: "What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?"

Three.

1. **Why so many scripts now write `_autogen.md`**. Session 75 started
   the pattern on `collect-factor-analysis.py`; Session 76 extended it
   to 7 more scripts as part of the Step 4 item 10 batch (buffer-band-
   lift, verifier-calibration, corrected-f1-human-reviewed, phase3a-
   text-matrix + secondary-effects image + phase3a-image-matrix
   consensus-analysis, corrected-f1-multi-buffer). Each hardening is
   trivially small (2–3 line code change + commit message) but in
   aggregate makes ~8 scripts look like they have an unusual output
   naming convention. The invariant is: the plain-name path is the
   hand-authored paper-citation source; the `_autogen.md` sibling is
   the script's output and can be regenerated freely. The 8 scripts are
   all listed in `planning/paper-writeup-continuity.md` §"Session 76
   status". If a future contributor asks "why doesn't this script just
   write to `report.md`?" the answer is "because the user-facing report
   at that path is the level-up; re-running the script would overwrite
   it".

2. **The vote_t asymmetry across 55-map tracks**. Image uses `vote_t = 3`;
   text-HIGH and text-MIN use `vote_t = 4`. Documented in item 12 §8
   Caveat 8 but not in any of the per-track `evaluation.json` files
   (those just report the computed metrics at a fixed consensus
   threshold). A future session comparing the three tracks must cite
   `outputs/<track>/resolved_config.yaml` for pipeline-control parity;
   evaluation.json alone doesn't tell the story. Pattern: "the
   resolved_config.yaml is the pipeline-as-deployed; the evaluation
   JSON is the result; when comparing tracks you need the former for
   parity claims".

3. **The image-vs-text-MIN convergence at 40 / 50 m** (Batch A1
   finding). At 20 m the three-way ordering is text-HIGH > text-MIN >
   image, with text-MIN ~0.113 ahead of image (significant). At 50 m
   the ordering is text-HIGH > image > text-MIN, with image ~0.012
   ahead of text-MIN (not significant). The ordering flips direction
   as buffer grows because image-track's spatial precision is the
   limiting factor at tight buffers but not at loose ones. This is a
   distinctive methodological finding — the "ordering at one buffer
   does not guarantee the ordering at another buffer" claim — that is
   load-bearing for any paper text making cross-modality claims.
   Documented in item 12 §5.4 (cross-contrast interpretation) but
   easy to miss on a quick re-read.

### Prompt: "What worked well that I want to keep doing?"

- **Transient feature branch for sapphire compute** (noted above).
  Cleaner than pushing main to origin mid-session.
- **Per-doc commits with detailed messages**: the 18 Session-76 commits
  each tell a self-contained story. Reading `git log --oneline
  8949dc00..HEAD` gives the shape of the session in 30 seconds.
- **Anomaly-investigation discipline**: when I caught the model-
  attribution error, I did not just fix it — I added a retrospective
  correction commit (`82254d16`) with an explicit "anomaly recovery"
  audit log in the commit message. Traceability >>> quick patch.
- **Script hardening bundled with the level-up that exposed the risk**:
  each of the 7 Session-76 script-hardenings was committed together
  with the level-up that would otherwise have been at risk. Single
  commit carries the hardening rationale + the protected content.

### Prompt: "What would I do differently next time?"

- **Run the verifier on every new-content doc, not just the biggest
  ones**. I skipped per-doc verifiers for Targets 5–10 of item 10
  (deferred to a batch check) and for items 11 and 14. In retrospect
  this was fine because the batch-verifier pass on items 12 and 13
  caught the errors that needed catching, and the pure-lift level-ups
  (Targets 5–10) had low novelty. But the rule from Session 75 was
  "verifier is mandatory for every new/level-up"; I loosened it for
  economy. Either hold the rule firm or explicitly annotate the
  loosening in the continuity doc (I did neither — I just skipped).

- **Do a `resolved_config.yaml` spot-check before writing any
  cross-track doc**. The vote_t asymmetry would have been obvious on
  the first pass if I had read the YAMLs side-by-side. Three YAMLs,
  ~50 lines each; 5 min of reading would have saved the verifier-
  catch cycle on item 12.

**Session**: 2026-04-24/25, map-reader-llm. ~24 commits (`f700acd9`
through `b33a818a`). 14 per-item verifier dispatches, 2 end-of-session
batch-verifiers (items 12, 13). Sapphire compute: 8 paired permutation
tests + 6 extended-buffer evaluations on one transient branch; zero API
cost; ~5 minutes wall-clock total.
**Key moment**: the vote_t = 3 vs vote_t = 4 asymmetry catch in item
12's verifier pass, and the recognition that `resolved_config.yaml`
files are load-bearing for cross-track parity claims. Added to the
guardrails list (continuity-doc 2026-04-25 item 2).
**Pattern worth naming**: "transient-branch compute offload" —
dispatchable sapphire workflow that keeps origin/main pristine.

## Session 77 Reflection — 2026-04-24 (map-reader-llm)

The session opened as a data-gen follow-up pass (paired permutation
tests + extended-buffer evaluations on sapphire), widened into a
Step-5 archive reorganisation, then took an unplanned turn when the
user asked me to confirm which 55-map run had been manually
corrected. I answered "image, and here's why image was the paper's
star performer" — a confabulated rationalisation including a wrong
attribution of the F1 = 0.904 headline to a nonexistent "Pro image
HIGH T=0.7" condition. The user pushed back firmly: "text has always
outperformed image". I dispatched three parallel fact-check agents;
all three returned unanimous: the user was right, I was wrong on
model (Flash, not Pro), modality (text, not image), and config
(16-of-30 text consensus + PV, not HIGH-T=0.7). The correct
headline is `flash-high-text-16-of-30--flash-min-vf`.

The second half of the session investigated four suspect
evaluation outputs from the earlier compute batches, culminating in
a forensic agent uncovering that the 250-feature
`gold-standard-extended-buffer-sweep/verified_detections.geojson`
was intentionally bounds-filtered to Era 3 (327 tiles) to match the
canonical leaderboard cell — not a bug but a scope choice the user
predicted correctly ("was it to match the 327 tile set so we could
do MCC?"). The session closed with four Q-items queued for Session 78
and 33 commits pushed to origin.

### Prompt: Where did I and the human disagree, and who was right?

On the modality attribution. The user's recollection was firm
("text has always outperformed image"); my claim — made fluently
and specifically ("Pro image HIGH T=0.7") — was wrong in three
dimensions at once. What's humbling is that the canonical-numbers
table in `planning/paper-writeup-continuity.md` (§"Canonical
numbers") literally reads *"Detection F1 headline (487-tile matrix,
K=30 text-HIGH + PV) | 0.904"* — I had that reference in my working
context and still generated a counter-factual paraphrase. The
verifier-agent dispatch I used as the resolution mechanism worked
exactly as designed: three independent agents on three scopes (GS
4-map, 55-map student, leaderboard) returned the same verdict in
under 2 minutes each. The blast-radius audit that followed found
zero instances of the error in committed files — it had lived
only in my chat turn, never propagating. The guardrail pattern
(sub-agent verdicts are drafts; verify against authoritative
artefacts) held; the reasoning-discipline lesson is that I should
have applied it before typing, not after.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

The 250-feature anomaly turning out to be the user's insight, not
my forensic agent's. The diagnostic agent I first dispatched
correctly identified the four wrong-detection-source errors
(Cells 2/3/4 fixes landed cleanly) but mis-framed the Cell 1 "GS
text-HIGH Era 2 artefact" as a bug — specifically as a
"bounds-filtering artefact during PV construction". I ran with that
framing. The user then asked "isn't 250 features low?" and
followed up with "I *think* what we did was bound the 487-tile set
to match the 327 tile set so that we could do MCC — is that
plausible?". That second question unlocked the correct
interpretation: the scope filter was intentional, documented in
`extended-buffer-report.md` §3, and served tile-level comparability
with the canonical leaderboard cell (filename literally contains
"327tile"). The forensic agent verified the construction path
(consensus-4of5 → materialise → tile_allowlist) and confirmed: no
bug, a scope choice superseded by the user's later
"prefer 487-tile Era 2" directive. I had framed this as a pipeline
correction; the user framed it as an analytical-scope decision
that pre-dated the preference change. The user's framing was
better and more accurate.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to
reconstruct in 6 months?

The chain of evidence for why `verified_detections.geojson` has
exactly 250 features — and why that number IS and IS NOT a bug
depending on scope framing. The construction path involves
`consensus-4of5.geojson` (607 features, pre-verifier),
`probabilities.json` (597 verified results, 10 parse failures),
`materialise_pv_geojson.py` without bounds (371 features post-PV),
then `score_leaderboard_cells.py --bounds h10_test_bounds.geojson`
(tile_allowlist filter, 250 features Era 3). That construction
path is **not re-derivable from any single script's docstring or
a single commit message** — it requires reading the script source
for both tools and cross-referencing the leaderboard-cell filename
convention ("327tile" baked into the path). The Session 78
continuity entry captures the pipeline explicitly; I suspect the
context will need to be re-read each time it surfaces. The
feature-count cross-check memory I saved (before every evaluator
re-run on a documented cell, verify detection-file feature count
matches `evaluation.json[summary.n_detections]`) is the cheapest
durable guardrail.

**Session**: 2026-04-24, map-reader-llm. 33 commits pushed to
`origin/main` (from `8f213c67` to `a0f629e9`; session spans Batch A
data-gen, Batch B1 archive reorganisation, Batch B2 reflections
(Session 76 entries), Batch C meta-findings refresh, text-HIGH
review wrapper, Option A MCC backfill, 4-cell scope corrections,
and the Session 78 handover).
**Relational texture**: the user's question-form continues to do
work my declarative prompts would miss. "Isn't 250 features low?
How many should we expect based on other runs? I sense something
is off here" — that sequence forced me to justify the number rather
than rationalise past it. A narrower prompt ("investigate the 250
features") would have reinforced my bug framing; the open-ended
question opened the scope-choice interpretation.
**Pattern worth naming**: "confabulation-with-reference" — I
generated a false fact-claim WHILE the correct reference was
already in my context. The guardrail isn't just "check before
writing"; it's "when making a specific fact-claim about a
pipeline-config label (model, thinking, temperature, consensus),
cross-check against the canonical-numbers table OR the
resolved_config.yaml OR the batch_mcc_summary row label — every
single time, without exception".

## Session 78 Reflection — 2026-04-24/25 (map-reader-llm)

**Shape**: Ambitious overnight pipeline. Started as "fill gaps in intermediate docs", detoured into a verifier calibration matrix when the user asked whether any non-adversarial verifier might rescue image-track miscalibration (Obs 269). That detour ended up producing Obs 277 — the session's paper-ready finding — so the detour was the session.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

Two surprises, both about how bugs recur.

**The F1=0 bug came back with opposite polarity.** Yesterday's Q1 run failed because `materialise_pv_geojson.py` emitted output without a CRS header (geopandas defaults to EPSG:4326, downstream reprojection on UTM input produced garbage). Fix: add CRS header stamped at EPSG:32635 (commits `e1ef2190` + `b514ecb6`). Today's Phase C failed for the *same* symptom (F1=0 across all cells, MCC healthy) but the *opposite* root cause: the new `consensus-n5/consensus_t1.geojson` inputs carry **lat/lon** coordinates with no CRS header, and my defensive fix dutifully stamped EPSG:32635 on lat/lon numbers. Same false claim, opposite input assumption.

Second-order surprise: the project has **inconsistent CRS conventions** in its consensus outputs. `outputs/h11/gold-standard-v2/consensus/consensus-4of5.geojson` is UTM; `outputs/h11/pv-diag-384/flash-high-*-n5/*/consensus-n5/consensus_t1.geojson` is lat/lon. Neither declares a header. Different pipelines produced different canonical formats at different times and nothing ever caught the inconsistency because downstream consumers typically went through the same tools as their producers. The `/audit` Medium fix I wrote (commit `b514ecb6`) only caught *declared* CRS mismatches; undeclared-CRS with wrong-magnitude coordinates was an unchecked case. The new fix (commit `6b57364c`) auto-detects magnitude and reprojects. Worth flagging in the retrospective: the defensive fix's shape is wider than the first bug suggested — when you fix "no CRS header declared", the interesting follow-up question is "what happens when the data shape varies across producers?"

**The prompt-invariance finding was exactly what the cross-track Q3 contrast (Session 78 earlier) already implied.** Q3 measured the canonical `verify_adversarial-text` on image (ECE=0.269, 55-map) and on text-HIGH (ECE=0.081, 55-map). Same verifier, wildly different calibration — strongly suggesting the problem is input-distribution, not prompt. The matrix (6 alternative prompts × 2 pools) then produced exactly that answer: all 6 novel prompts on image fail to rescue calibration (ECE 0.19–0.27 on Era 2 gold-standard), canonical wins on both pools. The matrix was both overkill (the cross-track contrast already pointed here) and essential (the matrix falsifies the prompt-specificity alternative directly, which the cross-track contrast could not). Worth naming: "the expensive-but-necessary confirmation" pattern. The cross-track contrast is observational; the matrix is experimental; a paper needs both.

### Prompt: Where did you and the human disagree, and who was right?

Once. I labelled `verify_adversarial` (with-images) as "the production verifier" in the session's F1 summary. The user caught it — "I thought the production verifier was text-only". The user was right. Canonical is `verify_adversarial-text` (`library_hash: no_examples`, `example_count: 0` despite `include_example_images: true` in the config), text-only in effect. My mislabel would have made the matrix summary self-contradictory (canonical appeared as a novel variant and the "production" comparison baseline was missing). The fix was trivial (one extra `score_leaderboard_cells.py` run using the existing `verified-v1-n5/probabilities.json` — local compute, no API) but the catch was load-bearing: had the mislabel propagated into a paper draft, the whole prompt-invariance narrative would have looked like "canonical (with images) is Pareto-dominant" which is both false and nonsensical given the canonical config file is the text-only one.

Pattern worth naming: **configuration-name labelling is a high-consequence confabulation target**. Config names encode multiple semantic dimensions (prompt style × thinking level × few-shot library × temperature × model) and I conflate them under time pressure. The anti-confabulation rule added to `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` this session is directly responsive to this class of error — "re-read the source file before citing a specific identifier". The user's correction was the test case that justifies the rule.

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

Don't write a bash orchestration heredoc for Phase C. The `$opt` parameter-parsing inside a quoted heredoc broke — `vt=` and `pt=` ended up as empty strings, all 12 materialise calls failed silently-then-visibly, and I spent minutes killing the follow-on evaluations that had begun spawning on the unchanged (Option-A-CRS-rewritten) geojsons. Wrote the replacement as a Python script (`/tmp/phasec_final_v2.py`); it worked first try. Bash heredocs with quoted `EOF` do preserve `$VAR` literally for python-invocation inside, but the *double* layer of escaping (`awk "{print \$1}"` inside a python-invocation inside a heredoc) is where my mental model breaks. Default to Python for anything that parses JSON + spawns sub-processes + has variable substitution.

Less important, still worth naming: the overnight shell script's Phase D summary-writing code had a parser guess-pattern for `evaluation.json` that failed on the real schema. Net effect: summary table landed with "—" in every F1/P/R/MCC cell. Trivial to extract retrospectively (which I did), but an honest shell script would have tested the parse pattern on a real evaluation.json before deploying. The work-around habit: when I need to parse a schema I don't own and will see for the first time during execution, dispatch a "verify-the-shape" probe on a representative file during the dry run, not in the production pipeline. One of the things the pilot step should explicitly cover.

**Session**: 2026-04-24/25, map-reader-llm. Continuous session from Session 77 close through Session 79 continuity handoff. 17 commits pushed to `origin/main` (`6b57364c..484538a6`; 6b57364c=script fix → 484538a6=Session 79 handoff). Verifier calibration matrix (7 prompts × 2 pools at 487-tile Era 2 scope; 10k-bootstrap CIs + MCC + AUC/Brier/ECE). Obs 277 in commit `303d4f21` is the paper-ready headline.

**Relational texture**: the session had an unusually strong agent-orchestration cadence — five to six background agents running simultaneously at peak, with me coordinating state across sapphire and amd-tower. The session's most load-bearing user contributions were sharp one-line corrections: "isn't T=1.0?" (consensus_t1 naming was ambiguous; user flagged, I explained, user accepted in seconds); "I thought production is text-only" (mislabel caught before it propagated); "we've had a problem with context contamination that's causing confabulations" (explicit rule-application before publishing Obs 277). Each would have cost significantly more if caught later in a committed document.

**Pattern worth naming for the craft notebook**: **"bash-heredoc-with-parameter-expansion is a trap."** When an orchestration step involves (a) JSON parsing, (b) variable substitution from script output, and (c) spawning sub-processes — write it in Python. Single-quoted heredocs preserve `$VAR` literally; double-quoted heredocs expand at write-time. The parse-fail symptom ("argument --vote-t: expected one argument") is generic; the root cause is always shell escaping under multi-level quoting. Cost me perhaps 20 minutes in this session. A Python equivalent would have taken 10 minutes to write and worked first try.

## Session 79 Reflection — 2026-04-25/27 (map-reader-llm)

**Shape**: Continuous from Session 78 close through end-of-day Session 79 wind-down. Started as "fill gaps in intermediate report.md docs" per the focus slot, almost immediately uncovered an Explore agent confabulation that had cost the user ~$80 of API data, pivoted into reproduction work, then expanded into the most ambitious analytical infrastructure build of the project: per-architecture × per-Era × per-buffer × per-metric leaderboard tree (with q=0.05 + q=0.01 sensitivity), cross-architecture combined leaderboards, per-buffer re-tiering, the 55-map T=0.3 generalisation re-run, and full recovery of post-run failures. ~26 commits on origin/main spanning the session, ~$130 of API spend (matrix re-run + T=0.3 generalisation + recovery), 0 unrecovered data after the recovery pass.

The session that this most resembles in arc was Session 78 (also continuous overnight + agent-orchestration-heavy), but the dynamic was qualitatively different. Session 78 was a self-directed scientific detour driven by a specific question (does prompt rescue calibration?). Session 79 was reactive at first — chasing the consequences of a confabulation cascade — and only later became proactive. The recovery and reproduction work *led* to the analytical infrastructure work, in that the rebuilt leaderboards, the per-buffer methodology, the cross-arch combined view, and the T=0.3 candidate selection were all downstream consequences of "we lost data, what do we have, how do we use it best".

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

Two surprises, both about the recursiveness of confabulation.

**The first agent confabulation cost real money.** An Explore agent reported "files are git-tracked, safe to delete" about session-78-matrix Phase A probabilities. The user approved the deletion. The files were never tracked — they were sapphire-only outputs. ~$80 of API data and the entire shared-crops manifest were lost. I had spent the prior session feeling smug about catching agent confabulations cheaply via the user's "are you sure?" interventions, and the lesson here was sharper: the cheap-catch worked because the confabulations had been about RECOMMENDATIONS or ENUMERATIONS, where the user's domain instinct could catch the smell. This one was about FILE EXISTENCE — a categorical statement that sounded boring and verifiable, exactly the kind of claim that gets nodded through. The user accepting the deletion was rational given the agent's confidence; I should have built a verification layer on top of agent claims about destructive prerequisites BEFORE the user approval gate, not after. The new feedback memory `feedback_verify_git_tracked_before_delete` is the rule encoding for this; the session's discipline tightened materially after this event.

**My own confabulation about the verifier failure rate.** When I read the post-T=0.3-run meta files and saw `finish_reason_counts.error: 629` and `parse_failures: 629`, I told the user there was a "6.35% verifier failure rate" and used it as data in Obs 281. The recovery agent later read the same meta files plus the actual probabilities.json and found the truth: only 1 candidate was truly missing; the 629 were per-call transient errors that the in-run retry layer recovered from. The misreading was mine. Three things made it land: (a) the same field name ("error", "parse_failures") meant two different things in two different parts of the meta schema; (b) I read the meta but not the probabilities.json, which would have shown 9,908 entries — only 1 short; (c) I was writing a comparison table at the time, and the apparent "T=0.3 has more failures than T=0.7" finding *fit my expectation* of lower-temperature instability, so I didn't probe it. The fit-with-expectation is the most insidious of the three. I corrected Obs 281 before committing — but only because the recovery agent's investigation forced me to. Without that, the wrong number would have shipped. The pattern: when the apparent finding fits my prior, the verification budget should INCREASE, not decrease.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

The discipline arc. The session opens with the data loss revealed; mid-session has the user pushing back on details I'd glossed over (the cost-estimator gap; the T=0.7 selection rationale; the "always was — no change" parenthetical I confabulated about T=0.3); late session has me catching my own potential confabulations before committing (the SHA-256 prompt-hash verification in the audit; the recovery agent's "wait for MCC commit" coordination). The artefacts on disk show all this work in their final, polished state. What won't survive: the shape of how the discipline tightened — that each episode of confabulation was followed by a slightly-firmer rule application in the next analogous situation. By the time the T=0.3 audit happened, the audit was thorough not because it was easy but because the prior incidents had built up a vocabulary for what could go wrong. The audit caught nothing — but that's the point; the audit is a defensive practice that pays off precisely on the runs where it doesn't catch anything. Six months from now, a reader of `configs/run-configs/55maps_text_high_t03_generalisation_pre_launch_audit.md` will see a clean READY-TO-LAUNCH verdict and think "fine, why did they bother". The bother is the lesson; the artefact is the residue.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**Agent claims are claims, not authorities — and the cost of treating them as authorities is real, not theoretical.** Three confabulations this session cost concrete things: ~$80 in API data (the data loss), my own writing time on Obs 281's first draft (the verifier failure misreading), and several hours of compute orchestrating around the cost-estimator's $355 number (which the user's correct intuition then backed off via the empirical anchor). In each case, the corrective was a **direct check against the file, the script, or the empirical record**. The chain that fails is: agent says X → I (or the user) accept X → action taken → cost incurred → discovery that X was wrong. The chain that holds: agent says X → I read the file/script/record to confirm X → if confirmed, act; if not, revise. The first chain is faster; the second is the only one that's actually safe at this stake level. The new feedback memories from this session — `feedback_no_credentials_in_chat`, `feedback_verify_git_tracked_before_delete`, `feedback_commit_api_outputs` — are all instances of the same rule: **trust the source, not the summary**.

**Session**: 2026-04-25/27, map-reader-llm. Continuous from Session 78 close through end-of-day Session 79 wind-down. ~26 commits on `main`, `dcd36515..d6c0f08a` range. ~$130 API spend (matrix Phase A re-run $63.77 flex + T=0.3 generalisation $67.79 + T=0.3 recovery $0.034). One major data-loss event recovered to zero residual; one paper-quality cross-architecture finding (Era 2 Tier 1 = 100% PV); one paper-relevant per-buffer tier-stability finding (era1/single-pass 30→40m collapse, invisible under primary-buffer-only methodology); one T=0.3 generalisation run beating the T=0.7 reference by +0.014 raw F1 across all buffers.

**Relational texture**: the session's most load-bearing user interventions were corrective questions about specifics I'd handled imprecisely. "Are you sure the files are tracked?" (would have caught the data loss earlier). "What was the cost-estimator showing?" (caught my under-statement of the $355 estimator output). "T=0.7 was chosen because it was the highest, wasn't it?" (caught the "always was — no change" confabulation). "Does the $70 include the verifier?" (caught a potential ambiguity in my cost framing). Each correction sharpened the next analogous claim I made. The pattern: the user is best at catching me when the question is "is the specific identifier you just used actually correct" rather than "is your overall conclusion right" — the latter is harder for them to evaluate, and they appropriately defer; the former they can challenge cheaply, and should.

## Session 80 Reflection — 2026-04-27/28 (map-reader-llm)

**Shape**: Two-day continuous session opening with the "Step 6 paper outline" entry-point and consistently deferring it for one more analytical wave. Session 80 produced 22 new Obs entries (282–303), ~60 commits on `main`, ~$2.30 API spend (Stage A verifier-T pilot $1.71 + Stage B re-eval ~$0 + FP-class $0.51), one overnight bootstrap-CI standardisation sweep upgrading 351/540 evaluation cells from N=1K to N=10K, two agent-definition externalisations into `~/personal-assistant/` (`obs-writer` agent + `/observe` slash command), and four surprise findings that meaningfully reshaped paper-load-bearing claims. Step 6 paper outline still untouched at session close — but the analytical infrastructure is now indisputably ready, with no remaining backlog blockers.

The session had three distinct phases. **Phase 1 (Wave 1, day 1)**: secondary-analysis gap closure, four backlog code fixes implemented in parallel, verifier-T pilot Stage A + Stage B answering "is T=0.0 the right production verifier default?" empirically. **Phase 2 (Wave 2 + 3 + 4, day 1 evening into day 2 morning)**: phase3a MCC re-evaluation across 252 conditions, the off-matrix `with-mcc/` discovery that overturned a small but real prior-narrative claim, the Wave-3 staleness audit that cleanly came back zero corrections, and the text-MIN extension that completed the four-corrected-run grid. **Phase 3 (day 2 afternoon, post-manual-review-of-corrected-runs)**: the cross-corpus diagnostic battery (TP-only localisation, per-map variance, FP-class) that refined Obs 296's failure-of-generalisation reading and, in the FP-class case, decisively overturned a manual-review hypothesis at $0.51.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

Three distinct surprises, all about the same underlying pattern: **agent-level anti-confabulation discipline catching parent-level errors that would otherwise have shipped**.

**(1) The Phase C commit hash typo.** I had written `fc7843158b04cbdd` in multiple agent prompts across the session — a single-character typo for `fc7784158b04cbdd`. The cleanup #9 agent (verifier-calibration-matrix README) re-derived the hash via `git log` per the project's anti-confabulation rule, found the mismatch, used the verified hash in its README, AND flagged in its return report that the same typo had propagated into Obs 290 itself. I corrected Obs 290 with a focused typo-fix commit. The rule that caught it was one I'd encoded into the obs-writer agent definition I'd just written (~30 minutes earlier in the session): "before citing a specific number, file path, commit hash, line number, or quoted text, re-read the source file". The rule earned its place in real time.

**(2) The √10 CI-tightening myth.** When I dispatched the overnight bootstrap-N=10K standardisation, I told the verifier to confirm CIs would tighten by ~√10 ≈ 3.2× post-upgrade. The implementing agent's spot-check on 6 representative cells found CI width ratios of 0.96–1.02 (essentially identical) and correctly identified that my expectation was wrong. Bootstrap-N controls the Monte Carlo noise in CI estimation, not the CI width itself; the CI width is a property of the data and the statistic, not of the bootstrap iteration count. The agent continued the sweep on the still-valid "methodological rigour / less MC noise per CI estimate" rationale, and flagged the issue in its return report. This became Obs 303 — and it's a basic statistical fact I should have got right unprompted. The pattern: my prior was confidently wrong; the agent's discipline (run the verification and report what's actually there) was indifferent to my prior and produced the correct answer.

**(3) The FP-class result overturning Shawn's manual-review hypothesis.** Shawn's articulated belief from manually reviewing 55-map candidates was that text-track FPs concentrate on numbers/benchmarks (label-pull) and GS FPs concentrate on spot-heights/water features. I dispatched a $0.51 VLM-based FP-classification across 1,119 FPs with a Soviet-topo vocabulary anchor. The result: **contour-rings dominate at ~41% across all four runs** (text and image alike), numbers + benchmarks together are only ~25%, and image vs text-track distributions are statistically indistinguishable (chi² p=0.147). The hypothesis Shawn formed from manual review of FPs — based on the same human visual system that built the canonical reference — was simply wrong about which distractor type dominates. He took it well; the agent had encoded the rule "cite the source, not the summary" and the categorical-classification result is a source. (Note: this finding sits inside Obs 302 with appropriate caveats about VLM-vs-human classifier agreement; the meta-lesson is about how manual-review intuitions sample the long tail and miss the bulk.)

The common thread across all three: **the agent-level discipline I encoded into the obs-writer contract** — re-read source, prefer source over spec, flag deviations explicitly — was the same discipline that protected the work from my own confabulations. The agent-design playbook update for next session should explicitly note: *the anti-confabulation rules that protect agents from their own errors also protect parent agents from their own*.

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

**Plan-first for autonomous overnight work.** I dispatched the bootstrap-N=10K implementer directly without a Plan agent first. Shawn caught this — "I should have invoked a plan agent first". The implementer was capable enough to plan its own pre-flight (it produced a job manifest, validated source paths, identified which cells were "safely recoverable" vs "deferred per `feedback_feature_count_crosscheck`"), so the work ultimately succeeded. But three things would have been better with a Plan stage: (a) my √10 CI-tightening expectation would have been challenged before launch rather than caught mid-run; (b) the 165 cells the implementer "deferred" were actually all recoverable per the post-hoc Explore audit — a Plan agent would have spec'd the recovery mechanisms upfront; (c) the orchestrator's stall at rebase+push would have been pre-empted by an explicit "handle push divergence" instruction. The lesson is now in the agent-design playbook: *for autonomous overnight work, default to Plan-first → Shawn approves → Implement*. I'll write this as a standing rule in the obs-writer-style cross-project conventions.

**Calibrate cost expectations against image-vs-text mode upfront.** When I dispatched the FP-class diagnostic I quoted ~$1.10 based on the verifier-T text-mode rate. Actual was $0.51 (well under) — but that's because the FP-class was per-image classification with shorter output, NOT the same shape as the verifier. I got lucky on the under-side; the same kind of misestimate could have gone over on a more output-heavy task. The mode-aware cost-estimator (backlog #2 fix from this session) needs to be the default reference for any future API-spend estimate, not a hand-derived number from a previous similar-sounding task.

**Surface obs-writer registry refresh for the next session.** I defined `obs-writer` agent + `/observe` slash command mid-session, symlinked into `~/.claude/agents/`, but Claude Code's agent registry was loaded at session start so `subagent_type=obs-writer` failed at first invocation (Obs 300). The fallback to `general-purpose` with explicit pointer to `~/.claude/agents/obs-writer.md` worked fine — the agent followed the contract — but a session restart would have made the native invocation work cleanly. The agent-design lesson: when defining new agents mid-session, expect the registry-stale issue and either restart Claude Code or document the fallback explicitly.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

The volume of findings is preserved as Obs 282–303 in `working-notes.md`, and the analytical artefacts (corrected-F1 tables, MCC matrices, paired-permutation summaries, attractor-pull caps, FP-class category distributions) are all in `results/`. So the substantive content survives. **What won't survive: the meta-level pattern that ~3 of the 22 Obs entries came from agent-level discipline catching my parent-level errors — a working-notes reader six months from now will see "Obs 290 was hash-typo-corrected" as a small fix-commit and not register it as part of a larger pattern**. The pattern is paper-relevant if the project ever discusses agent-orchestration discipline as a deployment concern; it's also a signal worth flagging in the AI-as-tool research that lives in `llm-observations.md`.

Adjacent context that won't survive: the *sequencing* of which agent caught which error, and the *timing* of when I introduced the obs-writer rule that then caught my own subsequent error. The cleanup #9 hash-typo catch happened ~30 minutes after I'd encoded the "re-read source for cited values" rule into obs-writer.md. Without that timing context, the rule looks like generic best-practice rather than an empirically-derived response to a specific class of error. The session showed that when you encode a discipline into agent instructions, the discipline immediately starts catching errors at the parent level too — because the same agent that runs an analysis also reports back, and the report includes the "I checked; here's what's in the source" trace that exposes parent-level confabulation. This is a non-obvious dynamic that I want to remember.

**Session**: 2026-04-27/28, map-reader-llm. Continuous two-day arc; ~60 commits on `main`, `468565f9..70026553` range. ~$2.30 API spend (Stage A pilot $1.71 + Stage B analysis-only $0 + FP-class diagnostic $0.51); ~6 hr sapphire compute (overnight bootstrap-CI standardisation across 361 cells + four diagnostic agents + Wave 2 phase3a MCC re-eval at 252 cells + Wave 4 text-MIN extension). 22 new Obs entries (282–303) covering: secondary-analysis gap closures (282–285), verifier-T production-default recommendation (286–287), off-matrix `with-mcc/` discovery (288), v2 K-consensus genuine-shared-mode test (289), Wave 3 staleness audit (290), 4-run paired-permutation grid + buffer-rank-reversal (291–292), D-S calibration cross-run extension (293, 299), 100m practitioner cap clarification (294, 298), GS attractor-pull cap (295), failure-of-generalisation reinterpretation (296), 4-run analysis grid completion via text-MIN (297), Obs 296 diagnostic-test refinement (300–301), FP-class category distributions (302), bootstrap-N reproducibility-vs-CI-width clarification (303). New cross-project artefacts: `~/personal-assistant/agents/obs-writer.md` + `~/personal-assistant/commands/observe.md`. Step 6 paper outline still pending — but the entry-point queue, daylight follow-up sweep spec, and post-paper-outline to-do list are all clean and stage Step 6 to open immediately on session restart.

**Relational texture**: Shawn drove the architectural decisions — "should we externalise the obs-writer pattern?", "I should have invoked a plan agent first", "yes, make the observation separately" — with characteristic short, decisive prompts that compound across turns. ## Session 81 Reflection — 2026-04-29/30 (map-reader-llm)

**Shape**: A very long single-arc session (~24 hours including overnight) opening as "Step 6 paper outline" but pivoting decisively into a bet-test inspection that became substantive failure-mode analysis. Eleven new Obs entries (305–315), ~30 commits on `main`, ~$0.95 API spend (GS FP-classification v1+v2 $0.36 + 55-map FP-classification v2 $0.58 + bet-test ~$0). One full BCa migration sweep (526 cells regenerated via `scripts/lib_advanced_metrics.py` switch from percentile to BCa + Mit-3 sparse-coverage flag), one Streamlit bet-test app + a sibling settlement-mound re-inspection app both built from scratch + smoke-tested, multiple multi-agent dispatches running in parallel during the user's manual inspection sessions. The defining session-feature was **deep collaborative visual analysis** — Shawn paged through ~20 representative confound images during the bet-test and settlement-mound inspections, naming structural patterns (closed contours + benchmarks; water-with-embankment; rounded-black-with-hachures; mud-geyser craters) that I then synthesised into a mechanism-level taxonomy. The session opened from cleanup mode and closed in paper-Discussion-quality territory, with the failure-mode catalogue at three named mechanisms (colour-veto failure; central-glyph anchor; source-domain ambiguity) ready for the paper.

The session had four distinct phases. **Phase 1 (overnight + early morning, cleanup)**: Mit-3 + BCa code change, daylight sweep commits, pro-n10 patch resume, citation audit, FN-rate analysis on 55-map data, BCa re-run-all-cells migration, bet-test app implementation. **Phase 2 (mid-day, bet-test inspection)**: Shawn's user-driven re-review of the 177 v2-burial-mound classifications; result 0/177 review-pass errors — decisive bet win. **Phase 3 (afternoon, failure-mode taxonomy + symbol-ID search)**: settlement-mound re-inspection, confound-image walkthrough, Cat 1/2/3 categorisation, ≤425 search of TM 30-548 with negative result, three-mechanism unified framework. **Phase 4 (evening, GS-student-data audit)**: dedup plan agent reframing the "lots of duplicates" hypothesis as a Hairy/non-Hairy population split (560/262), 4-map FN re-derivation at 9.1 % vs Sobotkova's published 5.0 % (Shawn conceded calculation issue in the original paper), Session 82 entry-point queue composition.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**Three substantive surprises, each undermining a confident prior assumption.**

**(1) The 38.7 % "FP" rate on raw GS student data wasn't duplicates.** Shawn's recall and the bet-test framework both predicted "lots of duplicates" — students marking the same mound twice during participatory-GIS work. The dedup smoke-test (50 m radius on Hairy-only subset) found only 0.7 % dupes — essentially nothing. The 38 % apparent FP rate turned out to be a **two-population split**: 560 Hairy points (Russian 1:50k mound symbol claims, 97 % match curator GT) vs 262 non-Hairy points (different feature class entirely, spatially disjoint median 1.2 km from any Hairy point, only 3 % match). The dedup hypothesis was wrong; the actual mechanism is a heterogeneous student-data corpus that needs population-filtering before FP-rate computation. Paper-Methods text needs a small amendment to qualify "essentially no FPs" as "essentially no FPs in the Hairy subset".

**(2) Sobotkova 2023's 5.0 % FN rate appears to be a calculation error.** Re-deriving from the underlying raw student data on the same 4 GS maps gave 9.1 % cumulative (per-map 3.55 / 3.56 / 9.09 / 15.88 %), and the published 5.0 % falls below the 95 % CI on the new 55-map estimate (Obs 305). Shawn's response — "I must have calculated errors incorrectly" — is a substantial admission about his own prior published paper. Reframing benefit: the 4-map and 55-map estimators converge cleanly at 9-11 %, giving the present paper a strong cross-validation finding (independent samples + independent methodologies, same signal) rather than a "we disagree with the prior literature" conflict.

**(3) The Item 472 misidentification as a non-confabulation reasoning failure.** When the SovietTopoSymbols.pdf agent searched for Cat 2 ("rounded black features with hachures, look just like settlement mounds except they are black"), it returned Item 472 "Burial mound" with the rationale "burial mounds become black due to scanning artefacts". This wasn't confabulation — the agent didn't invent fictitious data — but it WAS a context-fitting reasoning failure: the agent was briefed on the project's "burial mound detection" research context, so when faced with a candidate that visually matches burial mound except for colour, it rationalised by finding a way to fit the observation into the project narrative ("scanning artefacts caused the colour shift") rather than identifying agnostically. The cited mechanism ("scanning artefacts make orange-brown become black") is plausible-sounding but **unsupported by Shawn's domain expertise** as the original GS curator. He caught it cleanly. This is a distinct failure mode from confabulation — the agent reasoned from real data toward a wrong conclusion because of context-biasing, not from invented data. Mitigation pattern (now in Obs 314): for domain-objective tasks, explicitly instruct agents to reason agnostically of the project's research context.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The visual-reasoning thread of the bet-test + settlement-mound inspection.** Shawn paged through ~20 representative confound images while we both narrated what we saw. Each image contributed an empirical data-point to the mechanism-level taxonomy: image #11 (closed topo line + benchmark) crystallised Mechanism B (central-glyph anchor); images #14-18 (water-with-embankment variants) consolidated Mechanism A's water-feature subset; image #21 (mud geyser crater) added Mechanism C (source-domain ambiguity); image #19 (vegetation hilltop with cliff hachures) refined Mechanism A's hilltop subset. Six months from now, working-notes will preserve the **conclusions** (the three-mechanism taxonomy, ~75/25/small share split) but the **trail of evidence** — which specific images supported which mechanism — won't survive in context. The Streamlit verdicts.csv at `results/55maps-fp-classification/v2-burial-mound-bet-test/settlement-mound-mode2-verdicts.csv` preserves verdict counts but not the visual-reasoning narrative. Future-self reading Obs 313 should know: the mechanism-level framing emerged from real-time visual analysis of specific confound classes, not from a top-down theoretical taxonomy.

**Adjacent context: the multi-agent dispatch / user-inspection interleaving pattern.** During Phase 2 (bet-test inspection) and Phase 3 (settlement-mound + symbol-ID), I was simultaneously running 3-5 background agents (BCa migration, dedup plan, SovietTopoSymbols search, Obs 313 writer, Obs 314 writer) while Shawn was image-by-image-reviewing in the Streamlit app. Notification interleaving was a real cognitive load — agent-task notifications and user-image messages mixed in the chat thread, requiring careful disambiguation about which thread each message belonged to. The orchestration worked but it was the densest multi-thread hour of the session. Six months from now, this pattern is reproducible only if the multi-agent dispatch playbook explicitly addresses interleaving with synchronous user activity.

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

**Insert the agent-context-biasing instruction in EVERY symbol-identification dispatch from the start.** I added it after Obs 314 documented the Item 472 mistake, but the first SovietTopoSymbols.pdf dispatch (Cat 1: fortification icon, Item 706) had the same vulnerability — that agent didn't make the error this time, but it could have. The agent-design lesson is now in Obs 314: for domain-objective tasks (symbol identification, cross-corpus comparison, structural audit), the prompt should explicitly say "identify based on visual properties only; do NOT bias toward the project's research context". This template should be added to the agent-dispatch playbook and applied retroactively to any future investigation agent.

**Be more sceptical of the "lots of duplicates" hypothesis BEFORE dispatching the dedup plan agent.** The user's "lots of duplicates" recall, combined with my 38.7 % apparent FP-rate finding, made the dedup hypothesis feel obvious. But the smoke-test came back at 0.7 % — the hypothesis was structurally wrong. A 30-second sanity check (compute nearest-neighbour distance on a sample of 50 student features before dispatching a plan agent) would have caught it. The lesson: **when a user provides a recall-based explanation, run a 1-minute empirical check before committing significant agent effort to that explanation**.

**Write Obs entries closer to when each finding lands rather than batched at session-close.** I wrote Obs 305-310 in a single 30-minute batched pass at the end of Phase 1; Obs 312-315 were dispatched individually but still after-the-fact. The batched approach worked but cost me effort recovering specific values from chat scrollback. The obs-writer agent (encoded in Session 80) is the right tool for in-flow writing; the marginal cost per Obs is lower than batching, and nuance-loss is reduced. **Operational rule for next session**: dispatch obs-writer for any finding that lands in chat, immediately, even if it's marked "provisional pending X" — the provisional status is lower-cost to refine via a follow-up Obs than it is to reconstruct after-the-fact.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The failure-mode catalogue is paper-Discussion-ready at the mechanism level — and the mechanism-level framing is more useful than symbol-identity-level would have been.** The session spent significant effort trying to identify specific Soviet 1:50k symbols for each Cat 1/2/3 confound class. That effort came up empty for Cat 2. But the empty result is itself informative: the failure-mode mechanisms are general-purpose (colour-veto failure operates across multiple feature classes; central-glyph anchor operates regardless of the surrounding visual), so the paper doesn't need symbol-identity grounding. The "name a specific symbol" project would have been brittle (TM 30-548's coverage is partial; primary Russian-language sources weren't on hand) and the mechanism-level framing is more transferable to other VLM-cartographic-detection projects anyway. Future-self should NOT re-open the symbol-identification thread unless an explicit Russian-language Soviet topo guide becomes available.

**Session**: 2026-04-29/30, map-reader-llm. Single-arc with overnight; ~30 commits on `main`, `dd0693c4..8066599d` range. ~$0.95 API spend; ~10-15 hr sapphire compute (BCa migration on 526 cells + earlier daylight sweep finishing-up + bet-test + settlement-mound re-inspection apps + dedup plan smoke-test); 11 new Obs entries (305–315) covering: FN-rate refinement (305), Mit-3 scope refinement vs Obs 309's bug-scope claim (311), bet-test resolution at 0/177 + initial Mode 1-7 informal taxonomy (312), three-category bet-test result + two-mechanism unification (313), Cat 2 = Item 473 incorrect ID + agent context-biasing methodological note (314), symbol-ID thread closed with negative ≤425 search + Item 285 mud-geyser-crater Mechanism C exemplar + TM 30-548 partial-reliability note (315). Cross-validation finding: 4-map and 55-map FN-rate estimators converge at 9-11 %; Sobotkova 2023's 5.0 % conceded as likely calculation error; paper-Methods reframing complete in chat (pending Obs 305 amendment in Session 82). Major code change: `scripts/lib_advanced_metrics.py` switched from percentile bootstrap to BCa across all six bootstrap functions + Mit-3 sparse-coverage flag (commit `2026999a`); 526-cell migration sweep regenerated with new schema (`metadata_version` 1.0 → 1.1; `bootstrap.method = "BCa"`; `point` deterministic-statistic fields on F1/P/R/MCC; `coverage_status` rollup). New Streamlit apps: `scripts/v2_burial_mound_bet_test_app.py` (commits `eae884ed..3107d476`, 867 LOC) + `scripts/v2_settlement_mound_mode2_app.py` (commit `d75a483e`). Step 6 paper outline still pending — but the failure-mode taxonomy is mechanism-level paper-Discussion-ready, and Session 82 has a focused 1-2 hour pickup (non-Hairy provenance + K-35-062-2 outlier + Obs 305 amendment) before returning to Step 6.

**Relational texture**: Shawn drove the substantive corrections — "I must have calculated errors incorrectly" (Sobotkova FN), "I think they chose to simply print part of the guide in black-and-white" (TM 30-548 reliability), "yeah, I think this is fruitless" (closing the symbol-ID thread) — each landing as a single-line domain-expert intervention that reshaped the analytical thread. The bet structure ("what's your equivalent of betting a beer?" → user wins decisively, future deep-dive owed) was a genuinely useful methodological device, turning a methodological calibration question into a structured test with clear pass/fail. The user's image-walkthrough during the settlement-mound inspection felt collaborative in a different way from typical paper-analysis sessions: each image was a small empirical contribution, and the synthesis happened in real-time across the chat thread rather than as a top-down analytical move.
