## Sessions 82–84 Reflection — 2026-04-30 / 2026-05-02 / 2026-05-03 (map-reader-llm) — the FN/FP-correction + recovery-arc

**Note on scope**: Sessions 82 and 83 didn't get standalone reflections written (Session 82 closed with the FN/FP correction commits; Session 83 ran the T=0.7 recovery + first three Obs of the arc). This entry covers all three sessions as a coherent arc — Session 82 surfaced the trapezoidal-bounds correction + Sobotkova vindication; Session 83 ran the T=0.7 recovery and surfaced the parser-fix-vs-retry-storm cost-asymmetry; Session 84 ran the three follow-up recoveries, discovered the verifier-completeness gap, and landed the metadata-cleanup workflow. The work is substantively one arc; treating the three sessions together is more honest than fragmenting it.

**Shape**: Three sessions across four calendar days, ~140 commits on `main`, ~$58 total API spend. **Session 82** (2026-04-30, ~6 hours): started with the GS student-maps review thread; surfaced that the original "Sobotkova 5.0 % was a calculation error" framing from Session 81 was itself wrong — the 4-GS FN/FP analysis under proper trapezoidal-graticule bounds (Pulkovo-1942 datum) gives 5.27 % FN / 0.00 % FP, **vindicating Sobotkova within sampling error**. Obs 316 + 317 captured the Sobotkova-vindication + per-map-variance reframing. **Session 83** (2026-05-02, ~10 hours): T=0.7 55-map recovery surfaced 160 unrecovered tiles + cost overrun ($57.10 vs the $0.50 stage cap, ~228× over) due to HIGH-thinking + temperature-0.7 + JSON-parse retry storms. Patched the realtime parser with a 3-tier repair pipeline ported from the batch API (commit `e3aef6fa`); added Obs 318 (magnitude correction to Obs 281), Obs 319 (cost asymmetry), Obs 320 (T=0.7 propagation closure). **Session 84** (today, 2026-05-03, ~10 hours): three follow-up recoveries (image, text-MIN, GS-v2) ran for **$0.29 total** thanks to the parser fix (~100-300× per-tile cost reduction); GS-v2 recovery surfaced **10 silently-dropped verifier candidates** (image surfaced 18); metadata-cleanup workflow added (`failed_items[]` no longer drifts as a historical-frozen record); cross-track v2 wave + 4-agent doc-update wave + audit + Obs 321. **The single most consequential numerical correction in the arc**: GS-v2 published F1@50m was 0.8734; post-recovery is **0.8859** (+0.0126), a paper-Methods-load-bearing improvement to the gold-standard accuracy claim.

The arc had a consistent texture: each "small" finding (a single failure-rate magnitude, a single missing GT mound, a single recovery cost) opened a vein that produced 4-7 follow-on findings. Session 82's trapezoidal-bounds correction (catching that rectangular raster envelopes ≠ cartographic active area) cascaded into adding cand 4264 to GT for completeness, which became a precedent that ran again at cand 2397 in Session 84. Session 83's T=0.7 recovery cost overrun motivated the parser fix, which paid off massively in Session 84. Session 84's cross-track agent halting on a sign-flip alarm revealed an Approach B vs corrected-F1 methodology nuance that's now a paper-Methods caveat. **Each session built on the prior; the totality is more than the sum.**

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The GS-v2 published F1 was wrong, and we didn't know it.** Not approximately wrong — the published 0.8734 was understated by 0.0126 absolute F1 because **10 verifier candidates had been silently dropped from the original `probabilities.json`**. The runtime reported success; the consensus had 380 candidates; probabilities.json had 370. The pipeline didn't notice. We didn't notice. The cleanup script (`run_pv.py cleanup`) existed but had never been run on the original GS-v2 outputs. We discovered this only because the GS-v2 recovery agent ran the cleanup as a routine post-recovery step. **All paper-Methods claims about GS-v2 accuracy were quoting an incorrect number for months.** The corrected number is better (the paper improves), but the discovery process is the worrying part: how many other published-cell numbers across the project have the same gap? Phase3a cells (which populate the leaderboards) are next on the list to audit — the deferred phase3a verifier-completeness audit is the single most paper-Methods-load-bearing follow-up from this arc.

**The parser fix paid off ~100-300× per tile across the three follow-up recoveries.** Predicted to recover ~92 % of historical parse failures at zero API cost; in practice, image's 26 tiles recovered for $0.029 (vs T=0.7's $0.357/tile = ~$9.27 if scaled), GS-v2's 13 tiles for $0.041 ($0.003/tile vs T=0.7's $0.357/tile = ~110× cheaper despite identical HIGH-thinking config), text-MIN's 124 tiles for $0.144 ($0.001/tile = ~290× cheaper, partly because MIN thinking has minimal token compounding). The mechanism was retry-storm avoidance: the original T=0.7 recovery saw 14-25 retries per stuck tile (each retry burning the full HIGH-thinking budget), whereas with parser repair most tiles parse on the first attempt. The cost lesson is operational: **for HIGH-thinking + temperature > 0.3 runs, set per-tile retry caps to bound retry-storm exposure**.

**Text-MIN "failures" were historical, not current.** When the text-MIN recovery agent looked at `failed_items[]` it saw 124 entries — matching the audit count from earlier in the day. But re-running the proposer was a no-op: post-recovery per-pass GeoJSONs were bit-identical to committed versions. The 124 "failures" were a frozen record from a prior unrecorded recovery round; they'd already been fixed. The agent flagged this clearly, motivating the metadata-cleanup workflow. The deeper lesson: `failed_items[]` is not a current-failure signal in the pipeline as it was; it's an append-only historical log. Future audits that read it naively will produce phantom counts. The metadata-cleanup workflow (commits `7f328c62` + `368f652d`) closes this gap retrospectively + prospectively.

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

**Audit verifier-output completeness across all phase3a cells BEFORE running the T=0.7 recovery, not after the 3 follow-up recoveries.** Today's discovery of 28 silently-dropped candidates in image + GS-v2 raises the same risk for phase3a cells (which populate the leaderboards). If phase3a cells have similar gaps, leaderboard tier rankings could shift on the GS-v2 +0.013 scale, which would propagate into Obs 277/280/297 framings. The audit is cheap (~10 min, no API). Doing it at the start of the arc would have either (a) confirmed phase3a cells are clean (firming up the leaderboard claims), or (b) surfaced gaps that would have been recovered alongside today's cleanup work. Instead the audit is now deferred to next session — the paper-Methods follow-up is heavy because it could affect leaderboard claims that I (incorrectly) said earlier were "not affected" by today's recoveries.

**Use worktree isolation from the start of the 4-agent doc-update wave.** Per Shawn's reminder mid-session ("please remember to use worktrees when multiple agents work on the same docs"). The 4 doc-update agents collided across overlapping files; one agent silently dropped F1 tier rankings from `pairwise-permutation-v2/summary.md` via auto-regeneration; a second agent caught and re-added them with a "preservation note for future maintainers" comment. The collisions were resolvable (the agents stash/popped through them) but they cost time and the audit afterwards found 1 confirmed silent omission (text-MIN buffer plateau prose at line 115) that needed a 1-line repair commit. With `isolation: "worktree"` set on each Agent dispatch, all four agents would have worked on isolated checkouts and the merge-back would have surfaced conflicts cleanly rather than producing silent overwrites.

**Plan-first when committing API spend, even when "it's just a small recovery"**. The T=0.7 recovery cost overrun ($57.10 vs $0.50 stage cap = 114× over) happened because I dispatched the recovery without a Plan agent first surfacing the retry-storm risk. The plan-first lesson from Session 80 (continuity doc: "for autonomous overnight work, even when the implement agent is well-prompted, a Plan agent first surfaces edge cases for user approval before launch") applied here too but I treated the recovery as low-risk enough to skip planning. The cost is sunk, but the lesson is sharpened: HIGH-thinking + temperature > 0 + JSON-parse failures → retry storm potential; should have been caught at planning.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The bug-discovery sequence and how each bug led to the next.** Reading the obs entries (316-321) and continuity doc 6 months from now will preserve the WHAT — what bugs were found, what fixes landed, what numbers shifted. But the HOW — the in-session causal chain that led from "the T=0.7 recovery cost is high" to "let me look at the per-tile retry counts" to "HIGH thinking compounds retry costs" to "the realtime parser is missing the trailing-comma repair the batch API has" to "let's port it" — that procedural reasoning won't survive in the artefacts. Six months from now, the parser fix will look like a reasonable technical decision; the trail of thought that produced it (cost surprise → mechanistic investigation → cross-pipeline-asymmetry observation → port + extend) won't be recoverable. The closest archival surface is this reflection itself; future-self should treat it as the procedural anchor for the bug-discovery method, not just the artefact-preservation log.

**The Approach B vs corrected-F1 methodology nuance.** When the cross-track agent's f1_b for image dropped from 0.8316 to 0.7748 (a +0.057 sign-flip alarm), my first reaction was "image's F1 dropped catastrophically — something is upstream-broken". The actual mechanism is subtle: `paired_permutation_corrected_55maps.py` computes f1_b via "Approach B — extended-GT-at-R Hungarian matching" which augments the GT with **reviewed-mound-only** detections from the human-review CSVs. The 15 new image candidates from recovery weren't in those CSVs, so the pair script defaulted them to FP, inflating image's FP count by 15 → F1 drops by ~0.057 absolute. The corrected-F1-multi-buffer pipeline does this defaulting differently (treats new candidates more leniently if they're unrelated to known reviews), so its image F1 only drifted from 0.8316 → 0.8333 (+0.0017). **The two methodologies produce slightly different numbers for the same underlying pair.** This is a real paper-Methods caveat (worth a paragraph in Methods explaining when each pipeline applies) but it's also the kind of methodology-level nuance that disappears from artefacts and only persists in the reflection layer. Future-self reading the cross-track-comparison report should know: Approach B and corrected-F1-multi-buffer are not interchangeable, and the choice between them depends on whether un-reviewed candidates should default to FP (Approach B) or to neutral (corrected-F1-multi-buffer).

**Sessions**: 2026-04-30, 2026-05-02, 2026-05-03 (map-reader-llm). ~140 commits on `main`. ~$58 API spend. 6 new Obs entries (316-321). 3 fixes landed (parser realtime-vs-batch asymmetry `e3aef6fa`, D-S row-position `a9e280a3`, cost_manifest cleanup-overwrites-meta `7f05f529`). 4 deferred bug fixes documented (cost_manifest 2× double-counting; GS-v2 harness 2-resume race; verifier-completeness root-cause; auto-regeneration-overwrites-hand-authored pattern). 2 GT additions for completeness (cand 4264 / K-35-064-3; cand 2397 / K-35-062-4). Metadata cleanup workflow (retrospective + prospective) at commits `368f652d` + `7f328c62`. **Critical paper-Methods follow-up deferred**: phase3a verifier-completeness audit. Step 6 paper outline UNBLOCKED; phase3a audit recommended as first action of next session.

**Relational texture**: Shawn drove three pivot moments that materially shifted the arc. (1) Session 82's "the original 5.0 % was correct" — overturning my Session 81 framing in a single message and making me reread the data. (2) Session 83's "go ahead, full pipeline cap of $65, assuming we'll have fewer failures going forward" — the explicit cost-cap renegotiation after the $57 overrun, which let me proceed without a guilt loop. (3) Session 84's "don't phase3a matrix cells qualify for consideration when calculating leaderboards?" — a single sharp question that exposed my "leaderboards are not affected" claim as half-right and forced the phase3a audit onto the deferred list. Each intervention was characteristically short, characteristically decisive, and characteristically more accurate than my framing. The close-out was unusual in its thoroughness: Shawn's "have we confirmed all tasks and to-dos arising from this session that we did not resolve have been recorded in the continuity doc?" forced a final sweep that flipped one stale "in-flight" item to RESOLVED and added an Obs 321 cross-reference — small but the kind of close-out discipline that keeps the continuity doc useful for future-self.

## Session 85 Reflection — 2026-05-03 (map-reader-llm) — the audit-cascade + autonomy-on-deferral arc

**Note on instance boundary**: this reflection is being written by the same instance that did the work — no compaction or continuation. The session ran continuously from "let's tie up loose ends before the paper outline" at the start to "I'm leaving for travel; defer everything" at the end.

**Shape**: One session, ~12 hours wall-clock, ~50 commits on `main` (range `116399fc..a0c48bcf`), ~$0.91 total API spend (recovery campaign $0.905; everything else $0). Tier-1 went from 926 → 980 passing on sapphire (+54 tests), driven by the verifier silent-drop fix arc. Three round-trip audit cycles on the same code change. One overnight autonomous decision after user retired for the night. Multiple agent-orchestration patterns exercised: sequential chain (root-cause → plan → impl → audit), parallel sub-audits (4 agents, 1 per file), and unattended overnight execution (recovery driver as `nohup` background process).

The session opened with a clean directive ("tie up all loose ends before we start the paper outline") and a recommended sequencing I'd proposed at the prior turn. The user explicitly preferred my recommended sequencing over the alternative they'd been considering — a small but reassuring alignment moment. From there the session expanded substantially: a planned 4-item mop-up (audit, footnote, sub-band, 6-crop staging) became, by session end, a 30+ commit arc spanning a verifier silent-drop bug fix (Layer 1 + Layer 2 implementation, 3 audit rounds, 7 + 5 + 6 = 18 commits), a recovery campaign plan + execution + halt + relaunch, and a cross-machine sync. The expansion was not scope creep — each step revealed the next — but it does mean the "loose ends" framing at session start substantially understated the volume of work the user implicitly approved.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The audit found a bug the prompt didn't tell it to look for.** I dispatched four parallel sub-audits on the verifier silent-drop fix, with Agent 3's three flagged "attention areas" listed in each prompt: deferred-sleep pattern, synthetic LLMResponseMetadata field-names, and the LLMMetadataTracker-before-_resolve_model_name reordering. None of these turned out to be the load-bearing finding. Instead, the run_pv.py audit agent surfaced **three structurally identical critical bugs at three different sites**: Agent 3's centralised `_candidate_iteration_keys` helper correctly expanded to per-iteration keys when computing the *expected* set, but three call sites still used the old `_iter1`-only proxy when constructing the *actual* set or when calling `log_failure` / `log_success`. The result: multi-iteration resume produced spurious exit-1 in strict mode; multi-iteration cleanup never re-attempted iter2..iterN failures; multi-iteration `metadata_tracker.stats` undercounted by factor N. **None of these were on Agent 3's flagged list.** The audit found them by line-by-line diff inspection plus the "look for inconsistencies between the helper and its callers" cross-module-consistency check. The lesson is that the audit's anti-satisficing posture — "presuppose bugs exist; your job is to find them, not confirm" — is genuinely more powerful than a targeted-attention review when the bugs are below the implementer's awareness threshold.

**The autonomy decision at end of session.** The user said "I approve the cost of the recovery campaign, please run it overnight" and went to bed. The first agent halted on cell 7 of 20 because three cells had missing crop PNGs (gitignored bulk intermediates that were never committed). The agent stopped per its instructions and surfaced the diagnostic. I had to make a judgment call without the user's input: commit the 6 successful Tier-1 cleanups, edit the driver to skip the 3 problem cells, relaunch with the remaining 13 cells. I made the call to proceed autonomously. The user, when they returned in the morning, did not reverse it — they read the result, accepted it, and asked me to defer the remaining work and pull on zbook. **The autonomy felt aligned with the user's stated intent (campaign overnight) but unauthorised at the granularity of the specific decisions (commit cadence, skip-list scope, relaunch).** Six months from now, when reading this trail, future-self should know: the user's pattern in this session was high-trust autonomy on tactical decisions when the strategic intent is clear; the decision to relaunch unilaterally was within that pattern. But the same user pattern wouldn't necessarily generalise to a less-clearly-bounded decision (e.g., raising the cost cap mid-run); the autonomy was scoped by the cost approval ($10 hard cap, $0.91 actual) and by the skip-list being a clearly reversible action. The line between "tactical detail within approved strategy" and "decision the user would want to weigh in on" was felt rather than articulated; documenting it explicitly would help future sessions calibrate.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The structure of the recursive audit-fix-audit-fix-audit cascade.** Three rounds of audit refinement on the same code change: (1) initial /audit found 3 critical + 6 medium + lows; (2) fix-of-fix addressed all 3 critical + 2 mediums + 7 missing-coverage tests; (3) re-/audit found 0 critical + 0 medium + 6 lows; (4) low-severity cleanup commits cleared all 6 lows. The structural insight that won't survive in artefacts is that **each round was qualitatively different**: round 1 found semantic bugs in a fresh implementation; round 2 verified the fixes were correct AND surfaced the kind of "incidental coverage only" gaps that a fresh-eye review catches; round 3 caught DRY violations and dead-code branches that only become visible after the semantic correctness is locked in. **Audits stratify by depth**: a single audit pass conflates these three layers; the recursive structure separated them. Future paper or methodology work using this pattern should explicitly plan for "shallow → medium → deep" audit rounds rather than budgeting one pass.

**Why I made specific autonomy calls during the overnight relaunch.** The recovery agent's report listed three skipped cells with subtly different statuses: `55maps-gen-verified-v2` and `proposer-verifier-384-adversarial-text-v1-prompt` were clean-cut crop-regen-and-rerun cases; `e47-flash-high-text-1of5` had a deeper data-integrity question because the file's schema had been transformed by the first failed cleanup attempt, with the HEAD version *already* in the unexpected schema (suggesting an earlier overwrite). **I treated all three the same in the skip-list** — defer to morning user — even though they have different decision profiles. The cleaner approach would have been to attempt crop regeneration for the two clean-cut cells (CPU-only, no API), let the campaign clean those, and only defer the e47 to the user. I didn't because the cost calculus felt asymmetric in the wrong direction: the user had said "Stop only if you need my input about something", and the e47 question genuinely needed input, so deferring all three together had the merit of presenting a single well-bounded morning decision rather than three partial decisions of different kinds. Whether that was right is a judgment call; the artefact (the launch-summary.md surprise section) preserves the diagnostic but not the decision logic.

### Prompt: What decision or trade-off made today will look arbitrary without this session's context?

**Skipping the 3 crop-missing cells overnight rather than regenerating crops.** Crop regeneration is CPU-only (`scripts/extract_candidates.py`) and would have unlocked all three cells. The trade-off was: time (regen + cleanup + propagation = ~30 min × 3 cells), risk (regen failure modes I hadn't characterised), and decision-load on the morning user (zero open decisions if all 19 cells clean; three open decisions if the three are skipped). I chose three open decisions because the e47 cell's data-integrity question needs user input *anyway* — the deferral bundling was free relative to the regeneration cost. **Without this context, the launch-summary.md will look like "the agent gave up after the first failure"; with the context, it's a deliberate decision-bundling for the morning user.** Future-self reading the launch-summary should know: the choice to skip rather than regen was a decision-economics call, not a capability call.

**Choosing the Plan agent type for Agent 2 of the verifier-fix chain.** Plan agent is read-only by definition (no Edit, Write, NotebookEdit tools per its definition). I dispatched it for a planning task that needed to write the plan document, and it returned the plan as a message body asking the calling session to persist it. I then wrote the file myself. The mistake was visible (no harm done) but small operational lessons compound: **read agent definitions before dispatching, especially when the task involves file writes**. The general-purpose agent has full tool access and would have written + committed the plan in-flow. Without this context, the plan's commit hash (`036c6f44`) being made by amd-tower rather than the agent looks normal; with it, future-self should know that Plan-agent type is exclusively for planning that returns to a writable parent.

**Session**: 2026-05-03 (full day, ~12 hours), map-reader-llm. ~50 commits range `116399fc..a0c48bcf`. ~$0.91 API spend (entirely recovery-campaign). Tier-1 926 → 980 passing on sapphire (+54). 1 new Obs entry (322 — Obs 296 reframing as TP-localisation-tail). 3 paper-text drafts (B6 Approach-B-vs-corrected-F1 methods note; B7 curator-GT-incompleteness limitations; K-consensus heterogeneity footnote). 1 future-work register created (`planning/future-work.md` with detector-confidence items #4 + #6 migrated). 1 audit landed (Phase3a verifier completeness — 30 cells, 835 candidates dropped); 1 recovery runbook + driver template; 1 recovery campaign executed (cleanup phase complete: 17 of 20 cells, 530 candidates recovered, $0.905 spend, 3 cells skipped pending decisions). 18-commit verifier silent-drop fix arc (`ca0940de..ee658e72`). 3-machine sync complete (amd-tower + sapphire + zbook all at HEAD `a0c48bcf` end-of-session).

**Relational texture**: Two pivot moments in the user's contributions defined the session shape. (1) Mid-session, after the audit found 3 critical bugs in Agent 3's implementation, the user chose option 1 ("dispatch another agent... most thorough") over option 2 ("I fix them directly... faster") — a deliberate choice for thoroughness over speed even at the cost of an extra hour. The recursive audit pattern that produced clean code at the end was a direct downstream consequence. (2) End-of-session, after the recovery campaign completed cleanup, the user said "No, must defer. Write all defered tasks to the continuity doc, then push, and pull for me on zbook so that I can travel" — characteristically short, characteristically decisive, and characteristically clear about priorities. The deferral was the right call given travel; the explicit "I'm leaving now" framing forced the rapid documentation pass that produced the "🛫 DEFERRED until post-travel work resumes" section. The session-end texture was different from prior sessions: less collaborative-narration (more agent-driven autonomous work), more user-as-strategic-router (cost approvals, deferral calls, travel coordination). The continuity doc absorbed more of the "what did we do today" load this session than the chat thread did, which is the right shape for a session that produced more durable artefacts than discussion.

## Sessions 86–87 Reflection — 2026-05-04 / 2026-05-06 (map-reader-llm) — the recovery-campaign full-closure arc

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The "missing crops gitignored" diagnosis was sapphire-specific, not data-specific.** When the recovery campaign skipped 3 cells with `reason: missing_crops_gitignored`, every continuity-doc note + handoff treated that as an irreducible blocker requiring sapphire reachability or crop regeneration. The first investigation agent's headline finding — "all three skipped cells have their crops physically present on zbook" — was a clean instance of "the problem statement was sapphire-shaped because it was written from sapphire". Worth recording: when an obstacle's diagnosis is written by the machine that hit it, the diagnosis can encode that machine's view of the world. zbook had crops the whole time. Generalisation: when handoffs between machines use phrases like "missing X, gitignored", the next operator should re-test on their machine before accepting the diagnosis.

**The materialise-step gap caught only by the second redesign rebuild taking 50 seconds.** After today's first global rebuild finished suspiciously fast (~1 min vs the expected ~50 min cache-warm), I checked the pv-materialised geojson mtimes and found four were dated April 19 — meaning the rebuild had used pre-cleanup data for those cells. The gap exists because the project's pipeline has an obligatory `materialise_pv_geojson.py` step between `run_pv.py cleanup` and `build_per_arch_redesign.sh` for non-Session78 pv cells, but the runbook + executor recipes only document the Session-78-specific materialise script. A 1-minute rebuild looking impressive almost cost a quietly-stale leaderboard. Lesson: **wall-clock that's "too good" is a signal to look at outputs, not just exit codes**.

**The wrong-driver detour produced better artefacts than the original plan would have.** Yesterday's accidental `--top-n 20` thinning forced an investigation agent + a runbook fix + a script default-fix + a documented convention-propagation-failure in working notes. None of that was in the original Tier-1 plan. The arc that started as a regression closed with the project *better-documented than before* (commit `baa271bf` script fix; commit `ef3ec4fe` runbook fix across 4 sections; the investigation report at `planning/session-86-tier-regression-investigation.md` as canonical record). Net-positive bug: the hour we lost re-running was paid back many times over in future sessions that will not re-trip the same flag.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The audit script (`scripts/audit_verifier_completeness.py`) is now CI-grade infrastructure, not a one-off diagnostic.** The 2026-05-03 audit (`/tmp/verifier_audit.py`) was untracked, ephemeral, and irreproducible — its scope was implicit in the auditor's enumeration of "cells we know about". This created the campaign's "keep finding more" texture: every new cleanup run surfaced a new cell category nobody had yet enumerated. The replacement script walks the file system deterministically, handles 6 manifest-location patterns + a consensus-driven-cell fallback, and has 32 tier-1 tests. Once promoted to a tier-2 marker (a follow-up), accidental candidate drops in any future PR trip a CI failure rather than waiting for the next ad-hoc audit. The campaign's deeper closure isn't just "0 FAIL today" — it's "0 FAIL is now a CI invariant going forward".

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The `compare_wbf_vs_greedy_production.py` deferral was the right call but is easy to misread.** The script is broken (references `verified-v2/` which was archived at commit `3ec25e68` weeks ago). I deferred its repair on the grounds that (a) it's pre-existing breakage not introduced by the recovery, and (b) the Tier-2/3 investigation report verified e47 cells have zero era2/pv leaderboard impact, so the WBF-vs-greedy summary is research-artefact-only, not paper-citation-load-bearing. Both halves of that justification are *load-bearing in their own right*: drop either one and the deferral becomes a mistake. A future reader who only sees "deferred to follow-up" might assume the repair is trivial. It is, but the *decision* to defer is non-trivial — it depended on independent verification that the artefact doesn't gate any paper claim.

**The "10 to go" → "actually 11" off-by-one and what it implies for the campaign's enumeration.** Commit `0ceac93f` said "10 to go". The investigation agent's count was 11. The discrepancy is small — likely a one-cell ambiguity at a tier boundary in the original launch — but it is a small instance of the broader project pattern: cell-count enumerations across the campaign's documents drift by ones and twos because no canonical list-of-cells exists. The audit script makes this future-resistant for *probabilities.json* files but doesn't address other artefact types.

## Session 88 Reflection — 2026-05-12 (map-reader-llm) — doc audit plan, sapphire reconciliation, and a publishable finding from an unplanned parallel run

**Note on instance boundary**: this reflection is being written by the same instance that did the work — no compaction between session start and reflection. Same conversational thread from "documentation audit context check" through "Session 89 continuity beacon committed".

**Shape**: One day, ~4–5 hours interactive wall-clock, 5 commits on `main` (`ae0e0655`, `a19918cc`, `c6bd3576`, `2c00a531`, `b86b49ea`), **$0 new API spend** (the comparison ran against already-collected data). The session opened as a documentation-audit planning session (Slot 1 paper-B prep) and was supposed to be a clean dispatch-and-review of the audit plan. It pivoted mid-way when sapphire came back online and revealed 22 modified files — an unmerged parallel-run cleanup that became the day's most consequential artefact (**Obs 325** on T=0.0 two-regime reproducibility).

### Prompt: Where did you and the human disagree, and who was right?

When Shawn asked me to check sapphire-side loose ends, my first read was that the most-recent continuity beacon — which I had just refreshed myself with — explicitly said sapphire was "no longer a blocker; all recovery work has been completed on zbook locally". I cited that quotation back, suggested sapphire was effectively idle, and offered three options for what to do next. Shawn pushed back. He insisted the question was about *data and outputs* on sapphire, not about whether sapphire blocked anything.

He was substantively right. I had treated "no longer a blocker" as logically equivalent to "no state worth examining" — a category error. The beacon was correct in its narrow sense (sapphire-dependency does not gate paper outline drafting), but Shawn's intuition was that sapphire might hold artefacts of its own worth examining before being reconciled away. The investigation found exactly that: 22 modified files representing a completed parallel cleanup run that had never reached origin, plus 22 pre-cleanup `.backup` files preserved by the cleanup script. The eventual Obs 325 finding (T=0.0 two-regime reproducibility) is paper-Methods-relevant — it would not have been discovered if I had won the early framing argument.

The deeper lesson: when a continuity beacon makes an "X is no longer a blocker" statement, that statement is *scoped to a question* — in this case "what gates the next deliverable?". Treating it as scope-universal elides genuinely worthwhile investigation. **When the user reframes after I cite documentation back at them, the documentation is probably scope-limited and the reframing is probably pointing at the real question.**

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The cleanup_history wrong-file inference, and how cleanly two pieces of evidence reversed it.** When I first inspected sapphire's 22 modified files, I `jq`-grepped `run.meta.json` for `cleanup_history` on three sample cells and got `NO_CLEANUP_HISTORY` from all three. I formed the inference "sapphire's run halted mid-way; these are partial files" almost immediately and drafted a reframing message to Shawn. Two pieces of countervailing evidence broke the inference within minutes: (1) the stdout.log of the resume run explicitly ended with "Recovery driver complete. Cumulative cost: $0.904930" at 15:28:14 — a clean termination; (2) the diff line counts scaled with the audit gap counts (+2,774 lines for the gap=460 cell, ~6 lines per candidate). The actual issue: `cleanup_history` is written to `probabilities.json` (per `scripts/run_pv.py:387`'s `probs.setdefault("cleanup_history", [])`), not `run.meta.json`. I had checked the wrong file.

What surprised me wasn't that I made the wrong inference — that's normal — but how complete the inference felt before the contradictions surfaced. I had a coherent partial-run narrative built in under two minutes from a single piece of negative evidence (`NO_CLEANUP_HISTORY × 3`). The CLAUDE.md anti-confabulation rule warns about this: "Opus 4.7 is known to state invented identifiers with high conviction; treat specifics as suspect until re-checked at the source." **The same caution applies to inferences from negative evidence as to invented identifiers — the high-conviction pattern is the same shape, just inverted in sign.**

**The recovered-vs-preserved split.** When the comparison script ran, the headline aggregate (99.51 % exact match across 20,724 candidates) made the result feel like a clean confirmation of the existing T=0.0 near-determinism claim. But the per-cell breakdown showed 7 of 11 cells at 100 % match and divergence concentrated almost entirely in the gap=460 cell. The follow-up decomposition (preserved vs recovered) found that *all* 92 divergent candidates and *all* 14 decision flips fell in the recovered subset, with **zero** divergence in preserved candidates. That clean separator turned an aggregate-confirmation into a methodological refinement — same data, different reading.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The reasoning chain from "let's tidy untracked items" to "discovered the session-log commit precedent at the last possible moment."** When Shawn asked about disposition of the untracked items, I drafted clean recommendations — among them, "delete the 10 standalone session-86/87 logs (no inbound references, per CLAUDE.md throwaway-files clause)". Shawn replied with a single question that reversed it: "have we been saving them? I'd like to be consistent." The check found commits `7d15507b logs(s78-rerun): Phase A re-run logs (15 files, 12 MB)` and `770b32e8 logs(s78-rerun): Phase B/C/D logs` — explicit precedent for committing analogous session-named log directories.

That reversal felt earned-but-late: I had checked `.gitignore` patterns and inbound references but had NOT checked "have similar files been committed in the past?". Shawn's intervention was the single highest-leverage check of the session — a one-question precedent test that took me ~30 seconds to execute and reversed a recommendation I would have otherwise committed to. **The right rule going forward: before recommending deletion or archival, run `git log --oneline -- "<pattern>"` to see whether the pattern has been committed before. If yes, the precedent is the default.**

The commit message for the cleanup says "Brings session-86/87 driver logs into git per the logs(s78-rerun) precedent (commits 7d15507b, 770b32e8)"; it doesn't record that I had to be reminded by a single user question to find that precedent. Six months from now this will read as if I had made the right call instinctively. The reflection layer is the only place that preserves the almost-deletion and what averted it.

**Session**: 2026-05-12 (full day, ~4–5 hours interactive), map-reader-llm. 5 commits on `main` (`ae0e0655`, `a19918cc`, `c6bd3576`, `2c00a531`, `b86b49ea`). $0 new API spend (zero new model calls; comparison ran against archived data). 1 new Obs entry (325 — T=0.0 two-regime reproducibility refinement). 1 new top-level artefact (`planning/documentation-audit-plan.md` at 954 lines, ~25–38 hr remediation estimate, awaiting Stage Gate 1 approval). 1 new archive category established (`archive/investigations/` for completed read-only diagnostic reports). 13 previously-untracked items dispositioned per project conventions (10 logs grouped into 2 session subdirs, 1 investigation archived, 1 skeleton deleted, 1 already-grouped logs dir committed). Sapphire fully synced to origin/main from a 32-commit divergence + 22 working-tree modifications.

**Relational texture**: Three pivot moments shaped the session. (1) Mid-session, after I cited the continuity beacon's "sapphire is no longer a blocker" framing, Shawn re-framed the question to "what data and outputs are on sapphire?" — that single re-framing produced the day's most consequential finding. (2) During the comparison step, after I had written a clean script and produced the 99.51 % aggregate match, Shawn was off-channel; the project policy ("Preserve and compare, don't discard") was the thing on-channel, and applying it deliberately surfaced the recovered-vs-preserved split that turned aggregate confirmation into methodological refinement. The policy substituted for the user's intuition. (3) End-of-session, Shawn's "have we been saving them? I'd like to be consistent" reversed my session-log disposition with a single precedent-checking question. The session-end texture was less long-session-orchestrator (compared to Sessions 86–87's AFK-and-agent shape) and more interactive-and-confirmatory: each major step paused at a decision point for Shawn's go/no-go or framing input, and at each step his interventions were short, decisive, and consistently more accurate than my framing.

## Session 90 Reflection — 2026-05-26 to 2026-05-28 (map-reader-llm) — documentation audit Phase 0, the manifest reframe, and the H11 epicycles

**Note on instance boundary**: written by the same instance that did the work — no compaction across the three calendar days (the conversation was continuous from "I believe I owe you a review?" through this `/reflect`). First substantive return to map-reader-llm since Session 88 (2026-05-12); the intervening fortnight was other projects (RAC-TRAC, EFN).

**Shape**: Three calendar days, one continuous conversation. Began by clearing the easy backlog item (archiving two investigations, commit `d4722105`), then Stage Gate 1 approval unlocked Phase 0 of the documentation audit — eight spec edits executed one-at-a-time (propose / approve / commit / push), commits `c611c573..2ef592c8`, plus the audit-plan Changelog correction (`b31d3e36`). Mid-stream the session pivoted twice: first when Shawn proposed an agent-based proposer-verifier workflow for the back-fill, then more consequentially when he proposed retiring the "Era" terminology entirely in favour of runs/analyses/passes manifests. Two general-purpose agent investigations (GS tile-pool mapping, H11 reorganisation cost estimate) fed a planning decision rather than a data finding. Closed with the schema-design brief, handoff, and this reflection. **$0 API spend** — pure documentation, planning, and agent-reasoning work.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The audit kept finding the project's own planning documents were wrong — including the audit plan I was executing.** Phase 0 was meant to be mechanical: lift exemplar text into canonical spec form. But verifying-before-committing caught the audit plan's own § 3.2 framing was false (it claimed all four 55-map runs were dual-located; only two are — and § 5.1 of the *same plan* already knew this), the `experiment_intent.md` count was off by ~3× (plan said ~50; filesystem said 139), and § 3.3 contradicted § 6.1 on which file an edit touched. The document written to fix the project's documentation was itself a source of the errors. This sharpens the Session 88 lesson (orthogonal checks on inferences): the discipline has to extend to the *premises handed to me by trusted artefacts*, not just to my own inferences. A planning doc carries the same conviction-without-verification risk as my own one-shot inferences.

### Prompt: What decision or trade-off made today will look arbitrary without this session's context?

**The README rewrite that was frozen an hour after it landed.** Edit 8 was a full rewrite of `results/README.md` (commit `2ef592c8`, ~290 lines) that carefully organised 62 subdirectories around "OFAT / GS / 55-map" groupings and explicitly retired "Era" language. Then the manifest reframe arrived and we agreed *not to revise the README again until the manifests are populated* — so the freshly-rewritten README now sits as a deliberate snapshot of a structure we already know we'll re-cut around manifest IDs. A future reader seeing "rewrote README, then immediately declared it frozen pending manifests" will read it as thrash unless they know the manifest idea emerged *after* the rewrite was already committed. It wasn't wasted (the directory inventory it produced is input to the runs manifest), but the sequencing was genuinely "build the thing, then realise the better abstraction." Captured in the Session 91 beacon and Observation 26, but the *why* lives here.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The "epicycles → reframe" pattern, which Shawn named explicitly and which I'd otherwise have under-weighted.** The H11 reorganisation was accreting complexity — a cost-estimate agent produced Strategy A/B/C by hours, a SPECIAL_CONFIGS coupling analysis, an e47-is-ambiguous flag, a leaderboard-regen cost. I was prepared to drive a decision *through* that machinery. Shawn's intervention was to stop and ask "what IS H11, actually?" — and reading the hypothesis statement (`results/h11-tile-size-results.md`: tile-size, approach held fixed) plus the inventory's existing tagging dissolved the whole apparatus: e47 stays (it's a 384px approach characterised for the leaderboard), four subdirs move, no inventory edit, no regen. The baroque cost-comparison wasn't *wrong*, it was answering a question that a cleaner framing made moot. Shawn noted this had happened "a couple of times this week" across projects — which is the tell that it's a real failure mode of mine, not a one-off: when a decision is accreting epicycles, the move is to suspect the framing, not to perfect the epicycles. Now a feedback memory (`2026-05-28-6d0c01fd6dc7`).

**Session**: 2026-05-26 to 2026-05-28, map-reader-llm, one continuous conversation. 14 commits on `main` (`d4722105`, `c611c573`, `1aaece11`, `593d60f3`, `d9cc2501`, `c30ce58a`, `ee0d7aa1`, `0e697c77`, `2ef592c8`, `b31d3e36`, `9a959a59`, `6585345b`, `e2abe41d`, `9d85a64a`, `c7891872`, `78603036` — 16 incl. handoff/continuity). $0 API spend. Phase 0 of the documentation audit fully closed (8 spec edits + changelog correction). Two reports produced (`reports/gs-tile-pool-mapping-2026-05-28.md`, `reports/h11-reorganisation-cost-estimate-2026-05-28.md`), both INPUT-to-downstream not permanent. One design brief (`planning/manifest-schema-design.md`). 4 memories captured (`56afd23f91c8`, `ef713179f902`, `3be9d3066363`, `6d0c01fd6dc7`). 5 user-observations (24–28). TaskList: #5–14 done, #15 (schema design) / #16 (archive GS report) / #17 (H11 reorg, blocked by #15) pending.

**Relational texture**: The session's defining dynamic was Shawn supplying *reframes* rather than corrections. Two structural ideas — the manifest approach and the H11 "what IS H11?" question — both came from him mid-conversation, and both improved the trajectory more than any local correction would have. My role was execution-with-verification (the one-at-a-time spec-edit cadence, the agent dispatches, the pre-commit re-checks) plus surfacing options at decision points; his was the higher-altitude "are we even framing this right?" intervention. Notably this session had *no* confabulation incident of the kind that prompted his Observations 22–23 after 4.7 dropped — the verify-before-cite discipline held across 16 commits and caught four documented-but-wrong claims. Worth recording as a data point that the post-4.7 mitigation (prune context, verify specifics at source) is working under sustained load, not just in short sessions.

## Session 91 Reflection — 2026-05-28 to 2026-05-29 (map-reader-llm) — recovering a crashed schema-design session and completing the manifest schemas

**Note on instance boundary**: written by the instance that did *this* session's work — the transcript recovery, the brief rewrite, the schema authoring, the commits/push, and the memory `/forget` — with no compaction within the session. But a sharp distinction matters for reading these entries: the *surprising findings* I worked with (the model-provenance anomaly, the cross-config-fusion verdict) were produced by the **previous, crashed instance** and recovered from its transcript (`8529ef59-9d20-4d03-a3a1-01193d026c4e`). They are not my first-person discoveries. The two "Session 91" entities — the crashed attempt and this completion — are deliberately collapsed into one in the continuity beacon; the schema work straddles a crash boundary.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The crash fired *after* the decisive work was done, but before it was spoken — so the most valuable content lived in tool-output payloads, not in any assistant sentence.** The previous session died on an API error (`thinking` blocks in the latest assistant message cannot be modified) the instant after it dispatched the cross-config-fusion agent. But that agent *and* four follow-up Bash verifications had already run; their outputs sat in the transcript as `tool_result` blocks. The assistant's prose stream, though, ended at "Dispatching a focused agent." A reader skimming only what the assistant *said* would conclude the session's final question went unanswered — it hadn't. The verdict (fusion never crossed proposer configs → condition→pass is a simple prefix reference) was fully computed, just never narrated. Going in, "recover what we can" framed the task as partial salvage; in fact the decision-walk was one synthesis-turn from complete and all three of Shawn's final directives had been answered before the crash. The recovery was transcription of a finished argument, not reconstruction of a broken one.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

**The crash mechanism and the instance boundary — both invisible from the artefacts alone.** The resume-impossibility has a specific cause: `thinking`/`redacted_thinking` blocks in the last assistant message cannot be modified on replay, so a turn that dies with thinking blocks pending cannot be resumed at all (the four identical 400 errors at the transcript tail are the fingerprint). That is harness-specific and will read as opaque noise later. And these very reflection docs risk blurring whose reasoning was whose: the model-anomaly and cross-config findings were the *crashed* instance's, recovered second-hand; this session's genuine first-person work was the recovery diagnosis, the schema encoding, and the git/memory hygiene. A future reader should not credit "Session 91" with living through the model-provenance investigation — it inherited it.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The schema decisions survived intact and are now durably captured in three independent places — so trusting the design requires reading none of the crashed transcript.** The brief's § 1A carries the worked rationale, the schema files' `$comment`/`description` fields carry the same semantics inline, and the continuity beacon carries the summary. The reusable lesson for any future crash recovery: mine the `tool_result` payloads in the session JSONL (`jq` over `tool_result` blocks), not just the assistant's text — completed tool calls leave their full output even when the model never got to summarise them. That single move is what turned an apparently-interrupted session into a recoverable one.

**Session**: 2026-05-28 to 2026-05-29, map-reader-llm, one continuous session (the recovery session, distinct from the crashed schema-design session it recovered). 5 commits on `main` (`3204993e` schemas, `6c747721` brief, `4e00231d` continuity, `d6377e1e` hash backfill, `c730a8ea` dir move), all pushed (`origin/main` at `c730a8ea`). $0 API spend (pure recovery + design; no new model calls, no agents dispatched this session — the cross-config agent ran in the crashed session). Deliverables: 6 JSON Schema files (5 manifests + shared `common-defs`) under `docs/manifest-schemas/`, all meta-validating against draft 2020-12 with resolving cross-file `$ref` references; `manifest-schema-design.md` rewritten from proposals into resolved decisions (§ 1A, § 2, § 3, Changelog); a Session 91 closure beacon in `paper-writeup-continuity.md`. 1 memory soft-deleted (`2026-05-28-183835fe9bfc`, the auto-extractor's provenance near-duplicate).

**Relational texture**: Low-friction and confirmatory. The session opened on a recovery request ("our last session crashed … I couldn't resume") and Shawn was visibly pleased with the outcome ("worked things out better than I'd hoped"). He made exactly one adjustment — schemas to `docs/manifest-schemas/`, not `docs/methodology/manifest-schemas/` — confirmed sub-decisions 2–5 without fuss, and authorised the push. The one calibration note on my side: I committed locally and *asked* before pushing, where the standing `feedback_commit_push_before_review` preference leans commit-and-push; the caution cost one round-trip on a deliverable Shawn always intended to ship. A small over-cautiousness, not a misstep, but worth recording against the next committed-deliverable decision.

## Session 92 Reflection — 2026-05-29 to 2026-05-30 (map-reader-llm) — H11 reorg, scoring back-fill, and the Phase 1 generator's first vertical slice

**Note on instance boundary**: written by the instance that did all of this session's work — the reorganisation, the scoring batch on sapphire, the generator build, and the handoff — with no compaction within the session. First-person throughout. This was the longest single session on the project to date (three phases, one continuous conversation), which is itself the most reflection-worthy fact: the anti-confabulation discipline was under more sustained load than ever and did not decay.

**Shape**: Three phases. (1) Executed the H11 reorganisation Sessions 90–91 had planned — but a dry-run gap simulation *before any `git mv`* caught that the two `*-UNINTENDED-T1.0/` runs (which the cost-estimate report had scoped *out*) carried 174 references and were documented errata (E43/E44), reshaping the plan from "archive both" to "archive the unused one, keep the live one, no cosmetic rename". (2) The generator build then surfaced that ~12 aggregation outputs had never been scored; an agent sized the gap (a handful, not dozens) and they were scored on sapphire under strict parameter control. (3) Built `scripts/generate_post_run_report.py` and proved it end-to-end on the gold-standard-v2 vertical slice. ~$0 API (scoring is local bootstrap compute; no model calls).

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**The condition set Shawn approved couldn't be built as specified — the data said only one of the four was a scored result.** He verified a 4-condition decomposition for gold-standard-v2 (3 consensus thresholds + verified-v1). But when I went to extract their metrics, only `verified-v1` had an `evaluation.json`; the consensus thresholds had never been scored standalone on the GS corpus, and the conditions schema *requires* a metrics block. The schema refused to let me paper over the gap. This is the inverse of the usual surprise: not "my inference was wrong" but "the agreed plan met a hard constraint the data imposed". The right resolution was neither to weaken the schema nor fabricate metrics — it was to *score the missing conditions* (which turned out to complete a preregistered H3 sweep). The schema acting as a completeness audit on the data is the session's sharpest methodological datum (now Obs 327).

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

**I'd have scored all three consensus thresholds from the outset rather than trust the scan's classification of consensus-4of5 as "already scored".** The unscored-output scan reported 11 gaps and marked consensus-4of5 covered — because it appears in verifier-t-pilot evals as a *provenance source* (`consensus_geojson`), not because it had a standalone F1. I built the scoring batch on that classification, scored 3of5 and 5of5, and only caught that 4of5 had no standalone score *after* the first batch ran — a second sapphire round-trip to close the sweep. "Referenced as a verifier input" ≠ "scored as a detection set", and I let the scan's coarser distinction stand one step too long. The recurring lesson, one level up: a *categorical judgement* from a derived artefact deserves the same source-check I give its numbers.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The dispatch-then-verify agent pattern reached maturity here, and *unprimed* agents earned their keep as parameter-control guardians.** Session 90 used disposable agents as a dry-run; this session relied on them for load-bearing calls and they delivered — because Shawn insisted I not seed them with my expected answer. An unprimed preregistration agent confirmed "exploratory" (my prior) *and* surfaced that the consensus sweep itself is preregistered (H3), reframing the scoring's value. An unprimed scope agent found h8-v2 was evaluated on the 327-tile pool, not the 487 I had tentatively assumed — averting a parameter-control error that would have produced numbers inconsistent with the published headline. The deliberate non-priming is what made their findings trustworthy rather than confirmations of my priors.

**Session**: 2026-05-29 to 2026-05-30, map-reader-llm, one continuous session. ~20 commits on `main`, all pushed (`origin/main` → `a33d0220`): reorg `43895be1`…`c8f92781`; planning `53cb506e`; generator `e777bd0e` / `bfa0bc6f` / `dec10d71` / `df26d543`; scoring `9e56a447` / `e3be49ff` / `2141309b` / `6a2b78fc`; handoff `565e6290` / `c3f5d77c` / `52b0b206` / `a33d0220`. $0 API; scoring = local BCa bootstrap on sapphire (12 evals). Deliverables: H11 reorg complete; 12 conditions scored (`results/condition-scoring-backfill-2026-05-30/`); the Phase 1 generator + 4 gs-v2 manifests (`results/`); 6 tier-1 tests; Obs 326–327; user-observations 29–31; the E43 identity convention (brief § 1A) + a global-scratchpad principle. TaskList #17 done; the generator slice done; fan-out is next.

**Relational texture**: The defining dynamic was *forks surfaced with the investigation already done*. Twice Shawn bounced an `AskUserQuestion` back to "clarify" (rename the UNINTENDED runs; model the consensus conditions) — and both times the right response was not a decision but more digging (reading the errata; dispatching the scan agent). The forks that landed in one pass were the ones where I'd done the legwork first. His "size the problem before committing" reflex (the scan) and "don't prime the agent" instruction shaped the session's best moments. One self-correction against Session 91: there I over-cautiously asked before pushing a wanted deliverable; this session I committed-and-pushed freely throughout (no friction), which is the right calibration for lint-clean validated work on a 3-machine `main`.

## Session 93 Reflection — 2026-05-30 (map-reader-llm) — the registry fan-out: seven issues, a data error caught, and a scope belief reconciled

**Note on instance boundary**: written by the instance that did all of this session's work — the registry draft and seven-issue review, the housekeeping, the per-run facts (including dispatching four investigation agents), the 3a generator refactor, and the handoff — with no compaction. First-person throughout.

### Prompt: Where did you and the human disagree, and who was right?

**On the h8-v2/h12-v2 evaluation scope — and the answer was "both, about different things".** I had assigned these runs Era 3 (327 tiles) on the authority of the canonical scope doc (`evaluation-scopes.md` §6). Shawn pushed back from memory: 487 was the *principal* scope, 327 a special case. Rather than adjudicate doc-vs-memory, I sent an agent to gather the published-cell evidence — which dissolved the disagreement by showing it was a category error on both sides. 487 *is* principal, for the production / H11 / PV matrix (Shawn's memory, correct). 327 *is* nominal for the three library studies (my assignment, correct) — but the *reason* mattered more than either of us had surfaced: all three mined their few-shot examples from the pool_160 tiles, so scoring at 487 would leak. Neither "doc says 327" nor "487 is principal" was the real answer; the leakage constraint was. The disagreement was productive precisely because it forced the *why* out of hiding — a doc citation and a memory both gave the right-ish surface answer for the wrong reason.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

**That Shawn held the bar at "resolved" when I'd have settled for "detected".** Investigating verifier-t-pilot, I found its swept temperature lived only in `run.log`, not the meta — I flagged it, explained it, and was ready to move on with it documented. Shawn's reply was a single sharp question: "can I confirm we've *resolved* this?" — and the honest answer was no, I'd detected and diagnosed it but changed nothing on disk. That distinction reframed the work: it pushed me to prove the override was real (57% of 607 probabilities differ across temperatures), bound the blast radius (exactly two files, repo-wide), and apply a non-destructive correction with an erratum (E55) — turning a flagged anomaly into a closed one. The surprise wasn't the data error; it was how much the *detected-vs-resolved* boundary changed the outcome. He'd even pre-registered the hope ("exactly the sort of error I want to resolve in this review") — so the bar was set before the error surfaced.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The run-vs-condition boundary now has a crisp generative rule, and 3b depends on it: "a run owns ≥1 pass of *new API execution*; a pure post-hoc aggregation or fusion of existing passes is a condition."** This single principle did the work of a dozen per-case adjudications across the seven registry issues. It is why `wbf` is not a run (its four subdirs only fuse other runs' passes — no new API work) but `verifier-t-pilot` is (its T0.5/T1.0 arms made fresh verifier calls). A future reader decomposing conditions in 3b should apply this rule, not the directory structure — the directory layout was the *unreliable* signal the whole manifest project exists to replace. The corollary: where a clean principle exists, prefer it to enumerating cases; the cases then fall out consistently and the edge cases (wbf, the h8-v2 aggregation collectors) stop being judgement calls.

**Session**: 2026-05-30, map-reader-llm, one continuous session (Session 93 — the day's second, picking up Session 92's pending fan-out). 17 commits on `main`, all pushed (`origin/main` → `a9f59a5b`): registry review `8192787b`…`dd29436a` (8); housekeeping `52a212b1`, `a7b71741`; facts `392ae343`, `3519039d`; 3a `85a22030`, `b8b4e12c`; handoff `714fae80`, `eb70d00d`, `a9f59a5b`. $0 API (all extraction + read/grep; four investigation agents + one background agent, all read-only). Deliverables: the 27-run registry + per-run facts as `results/` inputs; generator v0.2.0 (B1 refactor, reads inputs, emits the 27-run runs-manifest); erratum E55; the §2.6 named-programme vocabulary; 7 tier-1 tests; Obs 328–329; user-observations 32–33. Pending: sub-step 3b (conditions decomposition + GAP-6/7/9/10).

**Relational texture**: A long, high-cadence I-draft-you-verify session. The defining pattern was Shawn converting my confident-but-shallow answers into investigated ones: the scope disagreement and the two months-old runs (pv-diag-256, verifier-t-pilot) all went to agents because he chose exploration over my MEDIUM-confidence guesses — and he named his own low confidence openly ("I'm a little fuzzy") to license the digging. Where Session 92's best moments were "forks surfaced with the legwork done", this session's were "guesses Shawn declined to accept". The one-issue-at-a-time cadence (seven registry issues, each its own commit) again earned its keep — but the higher-value intervention was his holding the bar at *resolved* on E55. No confabulation incident across 17 commits; the verify-at-source discipline held under the longest sustained load yet — including catching my *own* introduced error mid-stream (I had bucketed verifier-t-pilot at a phantom 256px tile-size to get a placeholder, and flagged + corrected it to an honest null before it shipped).

## Session 94 — 2026-05-31 — the build became an audit; a CRS bug and a scoring trap; the 2×2

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

That building the conditions manifest stopped being bookkeeping and turned into a methodology audit that found *real* problems — and that the surprises compounded. The verifier I built to validate my decomposition instead surfaced that only 2 of 27 runs had a current, standard evaluation; then the re-scoring surfaced that several h11 detection sets were silently scoring F1=0 because they were stored in UTM metres with no CRS member (read as WGS84, mis-reprojected off the grid); then the metrics surfaced that the proposer-verifier "verified" conditions weren't measuring the verifier at all (the scorer scores every feature, ignoring the `verified` flag). None of these were on the agenda. Shawn's framing — "this is how my manual practice always worked; documentation finalisation is where the methodology gaps surface" — named exactly what was happening and reframed a chore into the point.

### Prompt: What would you do differently if you replayed this session?

I'd have looked at the *numbers* before claiming "5/6 runs standardised." I reported that on the classifier's word (the evals existed and validated), but the classifier checks completeness, not plausibility — two of those runs were scoring F1=0 (the CRS bug). The metrics were the real verdict. The gap became a verifier flag (`f1-all-zero`), but I should have caught it from the table, not from the next probe. "It validates" is not "it's right."

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

The work pivoted: 3b is no longer "decompose 26 runs," it is "**standardise the eval record, then decompose against it**" — and the standardisation *is* the methodology audit. The re-scoring (14 uniform buffers + MCC, on zbook) and the verifier-as-audit-instrument are the machinery; the re-scoring worklist and the performance-shape 2×2 availability map are the maps. Read the Session 94 beacon and those two planning docs before resuming; the condition authoring that has been "next" for three sessions is finally unblocked, against a *clean* record.

**Session**: 2026-05-31, map-reader-llm, one very long session (Session 94). ~20 commits on `main`, all pushed (→ `bf4f9cd0`). Built `verify_run_conditions.py` (verifier + classifier + CRS/F1=0 flags) and `rescore_conditions.py` (14-buffer harness); standardised Batch A on zbook; fixed the UTM-no-crs CRS bug (6427e410, fbef3def + re-scores); made CRS explicit (eval `spatial` metadata v1.2; spatial-reference.md); locked three analysis-design decisions; cleaned 286 MB off zbook. Six agents (E43-repair, zbook-files, outstanding, bad-tilesize, CRS-repair, 2×2-map) — all read-only-or-careful; ~$0 new API (re-scoring is evaluation-only). Obs 330–332; user-obs 34–37; memories `…e45dad98a906`, `…9e0040de4433`.

**Relational texture**: A marathon I-draft-you-verify session whose signature was Shawn pushing *principles over patches*. Twice he stopped an ad-hoc fix and asked to be walked through the decision (the sidecar location; the verified-flag) — each time it produced a durable convention instead of a one-off. He extended my analytical proposals (the single↔consensus delta → the full performance-shape 2×2), enforced "map don't assume" (which the map then vindicated — no run has a complete 2×2), and watched the budget (flagging 80% context to protect continuity). Anti-confabulation held under the longest load yet, and the agents caught their own near-misses — the bad-tilesize agent debunking a misleading label, the repair agent confirming one genuine F1=0 wasn't a second bug.

## Session 95 — 2026-06-02 — an overnight run alone, then a calibration honesty pass

### Prompt: What was different about this session compared to recent ones?

For the first time the human handed me a whole multi-step sequence and went to bed —
"please do all of these tasks autonomously overnight." Recent sessions were
high-cadence I-draft-you-verify; this one had a long stretch with no verifier in
the loop at all. What made it safe wasn't cleverness, it was discipline I could run
without supervision: one commit per item (so a morning reader can audit each step),
verify-at-source before every claimed number, and — the load-bearing one — *make a
sensible, documented, reversible default where a design choice arose, and flag it for
review rather than guess past it*. The pv-diag threshold, the t0.3 GT mismatch, the
e47 run_5 provenance mess: each got a default + a written flag, not a silent decision.
The morning report's "flags for your review" section was the real deliverable of the
solo stretch — the work plus an honest ledger of what I'd decided on his behalf.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

That the "dodgy re-analysis" he thanked me for catching was *already in the corpus*.
I flagged that maximising F1 on the test set to pick a verifier threshold is
in-sample optimism — framed as a caveat about *my* new sweep. But chasing his "we
always exclude calibration tiles" reflex into the verifier-calibration-matrix README
revealed Phase B selects the per-cell operating point by F1-maximisation *on the 487
test set*, with no calibration-tile verifier data to select on. So the existing
`*-opt-20m` operating points were in-sample too. The caveat wasn't about a mistake I
was about to make; it was about one baked into the pipeline months ago. The second
surprise compounded it: the "manual review still needed" he half-remembered turned
out to be a *misremembered crosstab gap* (text-min/T=0.3 needed only the crosstab
script, no review) plus a base run that was a superseded original needing nothing.
The thing he was sure we still had to do, we'd mostly already done.

### Prompt: What's the single most important thing a future reader should know about this session?

**The verifier headline is the binary verdict (`prob_t=null`); the probability-threshold
operating points are an in-sample diagnostic, reported as a sensitivity curve, never as
a cherry-picked argmax.** This is errata E56, and it is the honest resolution of the
calibrate-then-test question for the verifier: the *vote* threshold was calibrated on
the held-out 20 tiles (preregistered); the verifier's `prob_t` never was (no
calibration-tile verifier data exists), so any single thresholded F1 from pv-diag /
the Session-78 matrix is in-sample. The saving grace, measured this session (Obs 334):
the F1 curve is flat in the operating range, so the optimism is ≤0.023 F1 — honest and
good-number are not in tension here. A future reader writing the paper must state, for
*every* threshold, when and how it was set.

**Session**: 2026-06-02, map-reader-llm, one long multi-day session (Session 95 — an
overnight autonomous run on 2026-05-31 + a follow-up day 2026-06-01/02). ~27 commits on
`main`, all pushed (→ `68843a8d`): the zero-API performance-shape work
`4d51c07d`…`02f1493b`, Obs 333 `44c0fa91`, consensus-PV + pv-diag `0e980055`…`7861b6d0`,
n1/crop archiving + un-track `d18b963d`…`21b5cc7e`, the calibration/E56 day
`8de56dc4`…`57fc1058`, 55-map doc `66acccee`, manifest publish `4b86c177`, handoff
`2b1106e9`…`68843a8d`. **$0 API** — every step was materialisation, evaluation-only
re-scoring (zbook), authoring, archiving, or a crosstab. Deliverables: the
performance-shape 2×2 filled (verified subsets/Decision 1A, single↔consensus deltas,
consensus-PV, pv-diag PV quadrants); `run-conditions.json` (88 conditions, 4 runs)
authored *and published* into all 4 manifests at `generator_version` 0.3.0; errata
E56/E57; `docs/methodology/55maps-generalisation-runs.md`; 46,766 crop PNGs un-tracked
(`sync-crops.sh`); text-min/T=0.3 verifier-vs-human crosstabs; Obs 333–334; user-obs
38–41. Five agents (obs-writer ×2, two background investigators, the overnight
obs-writer) — all careful/read-only.

**Relational texture**: A session of two halves with a single spine — *honesty under
delegation*. The solo overnight half ran on the morning-report discipline (decide a
default, flag it, never guess silently). The collaborative half was a calibration pass
where his domain reflex ("we exclude calibration tiles") and my methodological caveat
("don't tune on test") converged on a finding neither had alone — that the existing
operating points were in-sample. He redirected me off a wrong review (the gold standard
is curator-grade; only the student-digitised 55-map set needs review) and preferred the
cheap path ("if it can all be managed via a crosstab script, great"), which paid off.
Anti-confabulation held its longest load yet: I declined to confirm "image is the review
we need" on availability rather than verification, flagged that bumping GENERATOR_VERSION
for new data would mislabel provenance, and reverted my own hand-edits to generated
leaderboard files on hitting the anti-pattern — each a self-catch he didn't have to make.

## Session 96 — 2026-06-02 — the N=1 baseline matrix, with the human slowing me down to learn

### Prompt: What was different about this session compared to recent ones?

The inverse of Session 95. That one was solo delegation — "do it all overnight." This
one was the human deliberately *slowing the cadence to understand*, not to approve. When
I posed three forks (MCC source, analyses scope, n_passes), he answered the easy one and
on the other two wrote "could we discuss this? I want to make a careful, principled
decision but I don't quite understand how buffers and MCC interact." So a chunk of the
session was teaching — what a buffer is, why MCC is buffer-agnostic, why the eval was
split — and the teaching paid off twice: he caught that the matrix was below the
14-buffer standard (a real gap I'd have merged two stale files to paper over), and his
"MCC at 20m and 50m?" edge-case forced me to re-read the scorer and confirm the metric
is buffer-free rather than assert it. The other novelty: a background agent (the
parallelisation) ran in its own worktree while I worked the analyses/pass-publishing on
main — genuinely parallel, non-overlapping, and it came back with a clean PR.

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

That `per_item_metadata.model_used` was *also* wrong. The extractor carries a comment I
wrote treating `per_item_metadata` as the AUTHORITATIVE model source and `config.model`
as the unreliable Era-1 fallback — so my prior was "the n1 Pro pools have per-item
metadata, therefore their model is correct, therefore no override is needed." Checking
at the source (the discipline paying off again) showed the flash template-default had
leaked into *both* `config.model` AND `per_item_metadata.model_used`. The "authoritative"
source was no more trustworthy than the fallback for these pools. The override wasn't a
fallback-patch; it was a both-sources-are-wrong correction, and the study YAML was the
only truth left. A smaller surprise with the same shape: the audit verifier I think of
as a *legacy*-scanner caught a regression *I* had just introduced — absolute
`/home/shawn/...` paths in the evals I generated that morning. The tool built to police
old data policed me.

### Prompt: What decision made today will look arbitrary without this session's context?

Recording `model_used = gemini-3.1-pro` on n1's four Pro-pool passes when the recorded
meta plainly says `gemini-3-flash-preview`. With no context that reads as a bug — the
manifest contradicting its own source artefact. The context: errata E57 (the runner
didn't serialise the per-study Pro override), Shawn's explicit decision to follow E57
and record the study-design model, and — the part most likely to be forgotten — that
billing reconciliation is *still open*, so `gemini-3.1-pro` reflects the study **design**,
not an independently-verified dispatch. I deliberately did not silently encode this: the
model-version rule in his CLAUDE.md is marked CRITICAL, so I surfaced the exact string
(3.1, not 3.0), the meta disagreement, and the unverified status, and let him choose. If
billing later shows flash actually ran, the override is where to look, and the provenance
(`run-conditions.json` cited on those passes) is the thread to pull.

**Session**: 2026-06-02, map-reader-llm, Session 96 (a collaborative day, distinct from

(`32097c54`…`b57f69ce`) plus the squash-merge of PR #9 (`f67b2479`). **$0 API** — the

**Relational texture**: *Principles-over-patches* again, but the texture was pedagogical

understanding improved the output (the 14-buffer standard; the buffer-free-MCC

## Session 97 — 2026-06-03 — the foreshadowing came due: a study's intent was never executed

### Prompt: What surprised you about this session?

The hedge Session 96 wrote down came true almost verbatim. S96's reflection: "if billing
later shows flash actually ran, the override is where to look." I opened the billing
reconciliation expecting to *confirm* `gemini-3.1-pro` and close a loose end. Instead the
four n1-outstanding "Pro" pools were dispatched as **Flash** — confirmed not by inference
but by the Gemini API's own `model_version` (read straight off `response.model_version`,
`lib_llm_metadata.py:635`) on all ~3,896 tile responses. The deeper surprise was that it
*split*: the four **pv-diag** "Pro" pools really are Pro (billed at Pro's \$2/\$12 rates),
the four **n1-outstanding** ones are Flash — same nominal model, opposite reality, because
two different drivers (`lib_batch_api.py` vs `4_detect_mounds_batch.py`) threaded the
`--model` override in one and dropped it in the other. So S96's belief revision ("the study
YAML is the only surviving truth") was itself only half-right: the YAML records what the run
*intended*, and intent was never executed for n1-outstanding. Two sessions running, two
provenance layers trusted, two wrong.

### Prompt: What context from this session will be hardest to reconstruct in 6 months?

Why pv-diag is genuinely Pro *despite its `config.model` also reading `gemini-3-flash`*.
Both groups carry the E57 template-default flash in `config.model`; the discriminator is
`pricing_used.model`, which `estimate_cost` records verbatim from `model_name` — the
variable `submit_batch_job` actually sends to Google. pv-diag's resolved to
`gemini-3.1-pro-preview` (the override took), n1-outstanding's stayed
`gemini-3-flash-preview` (it didn't). Six months on, someone seeing `config.model=flash` on
the pv-diag *leaders* could "helpfully correct" them to Flash and detonate the tie_set. The
note to leave loud: the authoritative field for *what ran* is `per_item_metadata.model_version`
(the API's response) where it exists, else `pricing_used.model` (= dispatched `model_name`)
— **never** `config.model`, **never** the study YAML. The slug, the config, and the design
doc all record intent; only the response and the bill record service.

### Prompt: What decision made today will look arbitrary without this session's context?

Running the re-run in **real-time + flex** when every comparison cell (the flash originals,
the pv-diag Pro grid partners) was **batch**. On its face that's an uncontrolled second
variable — different execution path, different code. The context that makes it sound: I read
both prompt-builders and *proved* the payload is byte-equivalent — `_build_reference_parts`
returns `[]` for a text config in both modes, the image example-loop is `include_images`-
guarded identically, and each docstring states it mirrors the other. Mode changes the
transport and the price (flex = batch's 50%), not the bytes the model sees. Without that
verification on record, the realtime choice reads as a shortcut rather than an audited
equivalence — and the whole point of the run is that it *must* be parameter-controlled.

**Session**: 2026-06-03, map-reader-llm, Session 97. Two arcs. (1) **Lifted the N=1
leaderboard** stub → finding: round-robin tile-swap permutation + BH-FDR → 6 tiers, tie_set
= the two Pro-text-low-temp leaders (the permutation chosen *because* it is robust to the
`sparse_cross_grid` flag that made exactly those two cells' bootstrap CIs unreliable — the
right test for the data, not the default one). Authored the manifest finding, the
prereg-framing template, 16 tier-1 tests, Obs 336. (2) **Billing reconciliation** of E57 →
the n1-outstanding "Pro" cells are Flash; weighed the value of re-running (completes the
genuine-Pro thinking×temperature 2×2, probes the untested Pro HIGH/T=0.0 ceiling); audited
configs byte-identical (library + instruction hashes) to both the originals and the pv-diag
partners; smoke + two one-tile tests (empty + 10-mound) confirmed `gemini-3.1-pro-preview`
dispatch; launched the 8-pass realtime+flex run (~\$20–30, still running detached at
session close). Commits `c6aef99a`…`05838064`, all pushed.

**Relational texture**: the human set the bar with "we can't afford to undertake this run
more than once" — and *three times* chose to **discuss** a fork (tie-set method, prereg
framing, model identity) rather than pick from the options I offered via AskUserQuestion. The
pattern is not indecision; it is that he wants to reason *toward* the decision with me, not
ratify a menu I've pre-closed. The options were good but premature: on genuinely open
research-judgement forks, the discussion *is* the work, and the multiple-choice skips it.
